## AI Research

### [A basic case for differentially accelerating conceptual reasoning capabilities](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eimAN67tiNsrrtnbK/a-basic-case-for-differentially-accelerating-conceptual)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Introduction</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this post, we make the case that alignment and other important work require especially strong </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">conceptual reasoning </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">capabilities. By this, we roughly mean tasks that are similar to philosophy in that they aren't straightforwardly verifiable, and progress relies on informal </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a priori</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> reasoning and argumentation. We argue that differentially accelerating AI's conceptual reasoning capabilities is important.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also think it's neglected, and that the AI safety community currently underinvests in this area. We have ourselves pivoted from doing conceptual research directly to working mainly on differentially accelerating AI's conceptual reasoning. We have a shovel-ready agenda which can absorb more people. In a follow-up post, we will lay out at a high level how to work on this problem. If you are interested in doing this work, get in touch!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For others discussing related worries, see </span><a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2025/03/14/ai-for-ai-safety/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Joe Carlsmith on using AI for AI safety</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and on </span><a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2026/01/29/building-ais-that-do-human-like-philosophy"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">building AIs that do human-like philosophy</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/G4ARgcnFogpqorQgb/ai-doing-philosophy-ai-generating-hands-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Wei Dai on the potential difficulty of AI doing philosophy</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/HBxe6wdjxK239zajf/what-failure-looks-like"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Paul Christiano's first story of alignment failure</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8wBN8cdNAv3c7vt6p/the-case-against-ai-control-research"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">John Wentworth in his case against AI control</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and, for an overview, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/mskmgoE8HwgkD8hSo/readings-on-the-nature-of-alignment-research"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Caspar's readings on the nature of alignment research</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. See also </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/auMyz6pjPX5nGLYsL/the-basic-argument-for-making-ai-better-at-moral-philosophy" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Caspar's post on the basic argument for making AI better at moral philosophy</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which makes a similar case for that domain.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post gives a relatively generic case for working on conceptual reasoning, without focusing on a particular subdomain or path to impact. We do this partly because we think the benefits of conceptual reasoning are somewhat diffuse and the case for working on it is similar at a high level across subdomains, and partly to keep the post accessible to a broad audience. In our view, some of the largest benefits of work on conceptual reasoning come from improving the future's ability to navigate acausal interactions, but we leave the specifics of this for a future post.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overview</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At a high level, our argument is as follows. First, we argue that </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#It_s_important_how_soon_AI_is_good_at_conceptual_reasoning"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">it's important how soon AI is good at conceptual reasoning</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, because:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Important_challenges_need_conceptual_reasoning"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Important challenges need conceptual</span></a><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Important_challenges_need_conceptual_reasoning"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> reasoning</span></a><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many of the most important tasks have indispensable </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">conceptual </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">elements: questions that lack a practically accessible ground truth, that are in preparadigmatic domains, and that in various ways aren't well served by empirical data. (We say more about what we mean by this in this section.)</span></li></ol></li><li value="2"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#AI_R_D_and_takeover_rely_much_less_on_conceptual_reasoning"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI R&amp;D and takeover rely much less on conceptual reasoning</span></a><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI R&amp;D tasks have fewer and less crucial conceptual components, because these tasks are closer to being verifiable or have verifiable proxies. Thus, we expect improved conceptual reasoning to be differential, i.e., to help much more with safety work than with generic capabilities.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Takeover also seems bottlenecked mostly by non-conceptual skills, although stronger conceptual reasoning might accelerate some takeover-relevant capabilities, such as scheming. We briefly explain why we expect this effect to be smaller than the upsides, and leave a fuller discussion to a future post.</span></li></ol></li><li value="3"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#AI_being_better_at_conceptual_reasoning_earlier_would_be_very_valuable"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI being better at conceptual reasoning earlier would be very valuable</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We think it would be very valuable for AI to be better at conceptual reasoning at any point from now on, via two broad paths to impact:</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First, a central hope for overcoming the problems posed by superintelligence is to recruit AI's help. How early AI can help with important work, relative to takeoff and the onset of scheming, might be one of the most important determinants of the value of the future. This relative timing depends heavily on AI's conceptual reasoning capabilities.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Second, we expect that AI will soon play a very large role in forming the beliefs of future humans and AIs. Since society's views on some questions, such as decision theory and ethics, may be path dependent, we think it matters in the long run whether AI gives reasonable input and steers society in more philosophically reflective directions.</span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Second, we argue that </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#We_can_probably_differentially_accelerate_conceptual_reasoning"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">we can probably differentially accelerate conceptual reasoning</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, because:</span></p><ol start="4"><li value="4"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Work_on_conceptual_reasoning_is_likely_counterfactual_and_tractable"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Work on conceptual reasoning is likely counterfactual and tractable</span></a><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By default, models will likely be much worse at conceptual reasoning than they easily could be, for two reasons:</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Default training will likely generalise comparatively poorly to conceptual tasks. We expect AI companies to be able to get deep into the intelligence explosion by training mainly on relatively easy-to-verify tasks (e.g., finding learning algorithms with greater sample efficiency). Such tasks are a poor proxy for the conceptual problems we need to solve, which require strong reasoning in domains with very poor or no feedback loops.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI companies will likely neglect conceptual reasoning, since it is much less useful for AI R&amp;D and has few commercial applications.</span></li></ol></li></ol><ol start="2"><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even to the extent that default training instils the requisite underlying capabilities, models will likely remain underelicited on conceptual tasks.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We have also found this work tractable in practice: from our initial work, our sense is that basic elicitation techniques (e.g., few-shot prompting) and small-scale training can make models three to twelve months better on our main conceptual reasoning benchmark, i.e., improve their scores by roughly as much as several months of frontier AI progress.</span></li></ol></li><li value="5"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Effects_on_generic_capabilities_are_likely_small"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Effects on generic capabilities are likely small</span></a><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In addition to AI R&amp;D relying much less on conceptual reasoning, we give three reasons to expect that, in practice, work on conceptual reasoning will have small capabilities externalities. First, improvements to capabilities that advance timelines are less likely to be counterfactual, since AI companies' own training is likely to produce them anyway. Second, generalisation within conceptual reasoning is likely imperfect, so one can limit effects on timelines-relevant capabilities by focusing on less risky domains. Third, because generic capabilities are far from neglected, the downsides of accelerating them likely matter much less than the opportunity costs of not doing this work (see </span><a href="https://casparoesterheld.com/2026/08/25/a-simple-argument-about-capabilities-externalities-versus-opportunity-costs/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Caspar's post on this</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We conclude by briefly discussing </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Our_work"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">our work</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in this area. We will discuss how to accelerate conceptual reasoning (including conceptual issues around how to accelerate conceptual reasoning!), and which domains to focus on, in upcoming posts.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's important how soon AI is good at conceptual reasoning</span></h1><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Important challenges need conceptual reasoning</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We need to get many things right before and during takeoff, and we list examples below. We think many of these challenges have components of a kind we call </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">conceptual</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. That is, significant parts of the tasks are similar to philosophy in that they aren't straightforwardly verifiable, and progress relies on informal </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a priori</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> reasoning rather than trained intuitions, knowledge, or experimentation. We'll give more detail on what we mean by conceptual reasoning below. (That is not to say that the challenges below are purely conceptual — most involve other capabilities as well, some much more than conceptual capabilities.)</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Problems we may need to solve</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Deciding which research directions to prioritise within AI safety</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> — e.g., how should we prioritise between scalable oversight and control? Which promising research agendas has no-one thought of yet?</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Deciding which experiments will be informative in the long run </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">— e.g., will a given experiment give us insights that bear on the nature of AI risk, and why?</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aligning ASI to our values — </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">e.g., what should the </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/files/CEV.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ultimate</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> target of </span><a href="https://www.cs.cmu.edu/~conitzer/antschapter.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">alignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> be? Is it sufficient for humans to stay in control, or do we need to do more to ensure that the future is </span><a href="https://newsletter.forethought.org/p/we-should-hand-off-to-morally-reflective"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">philosophically reflective?</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Is a given alignment scheme fundamentally flawed?</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Modelling risks from misalignment and AGI </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">— e.g., how should we categorise alignment risks so as to think about them most productively — is the distinction between inner and outer misalignment the right way to divide the space? How much safer would it be if we only did pretraining?</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Deciding what properties to give AI — </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">e.g., is corrigibility good, and if so, what is the right way to operationalise it?</span></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Controlling (or making deals with) AI </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">— e.g., does </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/GCqoks9eZDfpL8L3Q/how-to-prevent-collusion-when-using-untrusted-models-to"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">untrusted monitoring</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> effectively mitigate risk from </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/bhtYqD4FdK6AqhFDF/fitness-seekers-generalizing-the-reward-seeking-threat-model"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fitness seekers </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">at the superintelligent level?</span></li><li value="7"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Determining how likely a given training setup is to produce scheming — </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">e.g.,</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">do </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YsFZF3K9tuzbfrLxo/counting-arguments-provide-no-evidence-for-ai-doom"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">simplicity/counting arguments favour or disfavour schemers?</span></a></li><li value="8"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ensuring ASI thinks reasonably about decision theory </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">— e.g., how can we avoid </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hfNBEKaStASAYMLiu/kimi-likes-causal-decision-theory-more-after-rl-in-twin-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">inadvertently</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2411.10588"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">training </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI to follow causal decision theory?</span></li><li value="9"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Deciding how AI should be regulated, and which international deals to push for</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> — e.g., are policies that make multipolarity more likely good or bad?</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Questions on which civilisation's views might be path dependent</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, such that the trajectory of our views before and during takeoff might matter in the long run (we discuss this more in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#AI_being_better_at_conceptual_reasoning_earlier_would_be_very_valuable"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a following section</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">):</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How should the future form its philosophical views? </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">E.g., how should we adjudicate between different reflection processes </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FSmPtu7foXwNYpWiB/on-the-limits-of-idealized-values"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to the extent that they give different outputs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">? When and how should we idealise agents' preferences?</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What decision theory should we follow? </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">E.g., how desirable is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ex ante</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> optimality? Is logical causation a reasonable concept? (We will explain in a future post why we think decision theory might be path dependent, and Emery gives some arguments to that effect </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5ro9kSxhP8mHXGmn4/against-strong-decision-theoretic-realism-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.)</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What values should we have? </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">E.g., how should we handle infinite ethics? Can we salvage person-affecting intuitions? </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Before explaining why the above challenges are partly conceptual, we define "conceptual" more precisely</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our definition is very similar to how Joe Carlsmith defines conceptual research </span><a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2025/04/30/can-we-safely-automate-alignment-research/#6-2-3-conceptual-research"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8wak8jgg4uv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8wak8jgg4uv">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, while some others use the term differently.</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For our purposes, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">conceptual</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> tasks have the following two properties:</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. Empirical or mathematical feedback is insufficient for learning to do the task well</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This means that the task is not itself feasibly mathematically or empirically verifiable and lacks adequate verifiable proxies. It also means that the task cannot be solved using uncontroversial reference class data (of course, in practice, whether something has a good reference class is often a judgement call</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref16c2u3pqocn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn16c2u3pqocn">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). The task may still </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">benefit</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from some available empirical data, but such data leaves some of the hardest parts of the problem unsolved. The task may also have less reliable or more subjective forms of feedback, such as having many people read a conclusion and argue about it, and seeing whether they converge.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most centrally, this includes domains with no ground truth at all, such as (in our view) ethics, but it also includes forecasting one-off "black swan" events, like AI takeover, where one cannot wait for an empirical resolution for practical reasons.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This criterion generally means that the task lacks established methodology: one cannot simply run experiments, write proofs, or do straightforward reference class forecasting. It also means that one cannot train on the task, or on any straightforwardly identifiable proxy for it, using RL with verifiable rewards (RLVR).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Feedback insufficiency also affects what it is like to do the task, in two ways. First, ground-truth feedback builds strong and reliable domain-specific intuitions. While one can also develop intuitions in conceptual domains</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefysjhtqbwj7r"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnysjhtqbwj7r">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, these intuitions tend to be less reliable (they may come from systematically unreliable sources of feedback) or leave more of the problem open. Second, the less verifiable a task is, the more one has to reflect, sometimes radically, on its success criteria while doing it. Indeed, solving conceptual tasks often involves "deconfusion" about what the problem even is.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. Progress is driven primarily by argumentation and reasoning.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is closely related to the previous criterion: one has to resort to argumentation and reasoning because mathematical or empirical feedback is insufficient.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This criterion additionally excludes problems where the part left unsolved by empirical and mathematical feedback is solved mainly by learned heuristics, gut feelings, or wordless judgement, rather than by argumentation and reasoning. For instance, art and design would probably count as conceptual by criterion 1 alone, but they are not primarily driven by argumentation and reasoning, so criterion 2 excludes them. Similarly, parenting is heavily informed by empirical data without being pinned down by it, so one might consider it partially conceptual in some sense. But what remains seems much more heuristic than, say, philosophy.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In practice, tasks often have a mixture of conceptual and non-conceptual elements, and both of our criteria are matters of degree.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One common reason for a question to be conceptual is that it depends on (human) values, inclinations, or propensities, i.e., on things partly orthogonal to instrumental rationality. For example, the answer to "what theory of normative ethics should we follow?" presumably depends on properties of human minds. (Some properties of human minds are cheap to verify; for instance, one can learn the current views of average humans by surveying MTurkers. But the properties we are mostly interested in generally are not. Many of them concern the outputs of reflection processes or of long computations that involve repeated input from human judgement, or at the very least require the judgements of smart humans who understand the relevant considerations.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefo9yy68j3u8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fno9yy68j3u8">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) We'll call questions like this </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">antirealist</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Some central examples of (arguably) antirealist domains are normative ethics, equilibrium selection in game theory, Newcomblike decision theory, and social choice. Arguably, some other domains with sparse data are also antirealist, because their answers depend on the choice of prior (or its real-world analogues), which itself seems antirealist. (E.g., consider the question of whether the Many-Worlds Interpretation of quantum mechanics is true, whether Tegmark Level IV is true, etc.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To give a better feel for what we mean by conceptual reasoning, the Venn diagram below shows where we think (or, in some cases, guess) various example domains fit.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 88.45118957703929%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/1a6a08dcea7966adef3a27efc58014eae66f0921aa6779a040ba6845fefd3d87/z0ezechdudytsouwqjep" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note: Forecasting ASI seems conceptual, but e.g., pre-AGI political forecasting seems like it has relatively good feedback (given that one can learn a lot from and practise on past data).</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We give more examples of conceptual (and non-conceptual) domains in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix__Examples_and_non_examples_of_conceptual_domains"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: Examples and non-examples of conceptual domains</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Joe Carlsmith provides a list of examples of past conceptual alignment research </span><a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2025/04/30/can-we-safely-automate-alignment-research/#6-2-3-conceptual-research"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftebrht53frp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntebrht53frp">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Returning to the challenges listed above, many of them seem to have crucial conceptual components (often alongside empirical or formal elements). We see three systematic reasons for this:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A lot of AI safety work involves reasoning about AI systems more generally capable than any human (e.g., ASI). Such systems lack an obvious reference class, and we have no clear way to model them.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For many questions, we can't afford to wait for empirical feedback, because it arrives too late (e.g., when misaligned AI takes over, or when we have prioritised the wrong thing for years).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Early reasoning on some of the above questions (such as those on the target of alignment, reflection, values, and decision theory) is important because of potential path dependencies. These path dependencies often stem from a question being antirealist, and antirealist questions are very often conceptual.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aside: One might object that reasoning about, say, ASI will become less conceptual as we approach takeoff, because we will get more empirical evidence about it. This objection is stronger if takeoff is somewhat slow. While we agree that such evidence makes questions about ASI less conceptual, we think it unlikely to come close to removing all of their conceptual elements. For one, in the case of events like takeover, how much one can infer from a system's behaviour in one setting about its behaviour in another (e.g., from an agent's behaviour in honeypots about its behaviour in deployment) seems like a fairly conceptual question, and in some cases it may be very hard to make strong inferences at all. For another, even when underlying capabilities improve gradually, behaviour may change relatively suddenly. For instance, misaligned AI systems may wait to attempt takeover until they have a good chance of success (e.g., they can afford to wait if they expect their successor to share their goals). Others have discussed at length how much we can rely on empirical evidence for alignment, so we won't pursue this question further here.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One might object that, while important work needs conceptual reasoning, it will be bottlenecked by other capabilities, such that earlier conceptual reasoning ability makes little difference. We think this is unlikely, for three reasons. First, many of the tasks above seem to be ~90% about conceptual reasoning, and many require much stronger conceptual reasoning than other capabilities. Second, significant progress is often possible even if other capabilities lag, and stronger conceptual reasoning can partly substitute for them. Finally, conceptual reasoning might be particularly neglected by default, and hence especially likely to lag behind other capabilities (see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Work_on_conceptual_reasoning_is_likely_counterfactual_and_tractable"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Work on conceptual reasoning is likely counterfactual and tractable</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI R&amp;D and takeover rely much less on conceptual reasoning</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Above, we argued that important work relies on conceptual capabilities. We now argue that AI R&amp;D and takeover rely much less on such capabilities. In particular, we argue that generic AI R&amp;D tasks have the two properties from our definition of conceptual tasks to a much smaller degree, that their conceptual components are less crucial, and that they are closer to being verifiable. Together, this section and the previous one imply that improvements in AI's conceptual reasoning are differential: they bring forward the time at which AI can provide a given amount of help with important work, relative to takeoff and the onset of scheming. In the next section, we argue that this relative timing might be one of the most important determinants of how the future goes. In a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Effects_on_generic_capabilities_are_likely_small"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">later section</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, we turn to a separate question: how much work on conceptual reasoning of the kind we advocate would, in practice, accelerate generic capabilities.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/8aaf84f84573c8b01727988192e6b94dd41c764413390c51e9c976a3151edf6c/nmjamuk6zesu0oaw5mxg" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This diagram is from </span></i><a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2025/04/30/can-we-safely-automate-alignment-research/"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Joe Carlsmith's Can we safely automate alignment research?</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and illustrates a view we broadly agree with of how conceptual capabilities research is compared to the domains we want to accelerate, like alignment research and philosophy.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We first discuss ML research taste, a skill that might bottleneck the intelligence explosion, such that improving it could accelerate timelines. We then discuss other partially conceptual skills that might accelerate timelines. Finally, we briefly discuss whether stronger conceptual reasoning would increase takeover risk, holding fixed the rate of development of generic capabilities. We leave a fuller discussion of this question to a future post.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How conceptual is ML research taste in generic capabilities contexts?</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let's take "taste" to include issues on which one makes progress by argumentation, since to the extent that we view “taste” as a very gut-level thing, it’s different from conceptual reasoning as we defined it on very direct methodological grounds. Even so, we think ML research taste is mostly non-conceptual, because its most relevant form</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcl82x3gh7go"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncl82x3gh7go">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> consists of predictions about effectiveness, on which one gets a decent amount of feedback.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgmttq1u6ovw"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngmttq1u6ovw">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> For example, predictions about:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">what things will and won’t work;</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">what will be cumbersome or too fiddly to work with;</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">whether an experiment will have a clear conclusion;</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">what will be informative about future training, what will scale, and so on.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even where ML research taste isn't directly verifiable, it is trained by lots of feedback on verifiable tasks, and it relies more on heuristics and intuitions from experience than on </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a priori</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> reasoning about out-of-distribution topics. We therefore think that ML research taste can mostly be learned from empirical feedback (e.g., via RLVR), much more so than the tasks we discussed above.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It also seems like most capabilities work is mostly about current capabilities, and doesn't require (much) reasoning about superintelligence, in contrast to a lot of AI safety work (see discussion above). Moreover, antirealist questions (as described above) seem unimportant for capabilities research.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The big caveat is that some things might be predictive for superintelligence in particular, in a way that can’t be viewed as a continuous extension of other kinds of prediction. For instance, someone might have a “taste” favouring research directions that are closer to human intelligence (e.g., preferring more brain-like neural network architectures, preferring multimodal models over pure LLMs), mostly with an eye towards AGI. Such taste — and debates about whether it is reasonable — seems somewhat conceptual, because it doesn’t hinge that much on making near-term predictions. However, we think big conceptual insights like this probably don't bottleneck AI R&amp;D much. We discuss this more in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix__is_ML_bottlenecked_by_conceptual_insights_"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: is ML bottlenecked by conceptual insights?</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In empirical support of our view that conceptual reasoning is relatively unimportant for ML research, we note that AI companies also seem to select ML researchers relatively little for conceptual reasoning ability, compared with skill at narrower ML research tasks.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefyioqfwmwwgl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnyioqfwmwwgl">[8]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One possible objection is that AI companies will pick the low-hanging fruit from approaches like RLVR, such that the remaining bottlenecks to AI R&amp;D will be more conceptual, because conceptual skills are harder to train with such approaches.</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the most extreme case, some conceptual tasks might be essentially necessary for the intelligence explosion to progress past a given point.</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We agree that this effect makes improving conceptual reasoning more valuable for AI R&amp;D, in relation to RLVR, than it currently is. But we find it fairly implausible that conceptual reasoning will bottleneck AI R&amp;D as a result.</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In particular, we don't expect improving AI's conceptual reasoning to become worth a significant fraction of AI companies' resources, and we expect it to remain an ineffective way to accelerate AI R&amp;D.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other timelines-relevant capabilities that might be conceptual</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consider the task of forecasting the intelligence explosion. Some aspects of this task seem conceptual, because the intelligence explosion is so unprecedented. AI being better at this task could shorten timelines, for instance by helping AI companies allocate resources better. But it also has upsides: better forecasts of timelines and risks would improve our ability to prepare and make the public better informed (though they might also make governments more inclined to race). Similarly, while strategic reasoning is typically well served by empirical feedback, strategic reasoning about the intelligence explosion seems partly conceptual, and improving it would have both upsides and downsides. If these were the only effects of improved conceptual reasoning, the sign would be less clear. For this reason, we currently don't recommend focusing on generic versions of these two capabilities. But we think the other benefits of conceptual reasoning outweigh these possible downsides.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Accelerating scheming</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another concern is that stronger conceptual reasoning would also accelerate capabilities relevant to takeover, such as scheming, breaking out of control schemes, or (mis)aligning successors. We think this is a much more important downside than the effect on timelines. AI companies are already trying hard to accelerate generic capabilities, which makes our counterfactual effect on those capabilities small (see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/editPost?postId=eimAN67tiNsrrtnbK&amp;key=8f124316b44f1f962d6f072fd43752#Effects_on_generic_capabilities_are_likely_small" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Effects on generic capabilities are likely small</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), but they are not trying to accelerate takeover-specific capabilities. Nonetheless, we expect the effect on takeover-relevant capabilities to be smaller than the upsides. This concern informs which conceptual reasoning domains we recommend focusing on; for instance, we think one should likely avoid accelerating control red-teaming. The topic deserves a fuller discussion than we can give it here, and we intend to provide one in a future post. We outline some thoughts below:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We expect improved conceptual reasoning ability to have little effect on whether the AI is scheming, and only a modest effect on whether it is a coherent schemer or generically good at strategic reasoning. This is largely because other parts of training seem to train generic strategic reasoning and scheming much more strongly, and these skills seem mostly non-conceptual.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We think takeover is bottlenecked mostly by non-conceptual skills. It seems to need strong domain-specific skills (e.g., bio, cyber, persuasion) and an understanding of civilisation. Its less verifiable parts look more like generic real-world strategy, and AI companies already have strong incentives to train this.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We do worry about improving AI's ability to reason about what control measures it might be under, and about ways to subvert them. While stronger control reasoning also improves the AI's ability to come up with control schemes, this ability is less likely to help us if the AI is already scheming, because it may then sandbag on this work.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We do worry about improving AI's ability to align its successors with itself. We think this effect probably somewhat reduces the value of making AI better at alignment, but we think it is quite unlikely to make that value negative. If early AI is not scheming, improving its ability to help with AI safety work seems clearly good. If it is scheming, the improvement might be harmful, but this seems less clear, or at least any harm seems smaller than the benefit in worlds without early scheming. For one, it's then quite likely that things will go badly by default. For another, the improvement still has positive effects if we can prevent sandbagging, e.g., by training the AI on high-quality datasets for the tasks we care about (see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TeTegzR8X5CuKgMc3/misalignment-and-strategic-underperformance-an-analysis-of"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for a discussion of this). For instance, if a scheming AI can't sandbag, then its improved understanding of alignment should also improve our own understanding via the research it does for us. A better understanding of alignment (e.g., of when training produces schemers) could in turn make us more likely to discover that the AI is scheming. Finally, a lot of alignment work is somewhat specific to alignment with human values. The work a misaligned AI has to do to align its successor might be quite different, and perhaps less conceptual (for instance, one difference is that misaligned AI already has the information that its training procedure led to its values).</span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI being better at conceptual reasoning earlier would be very valuable</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Perhaps the most important determinant of how well we do on the challenges we listed in the first section is how well, and how early relative to takeoff, AI can assist with them. Since these challenges seem to need conceptual reasoning much more than AI development does (see the previous two sections), the timing of this assistance relative to takeoff depends heavily on AI's conceptual reasoning capabilities. Thus, AI being better at conceptual reasoning earlier helps via two paths to impact:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Having enough time to solve the important problems:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Once AI is at expert-human level at some category of important work, its output will very likely dwarf unassisted human output. Therefore, a major determinant of whether we get the relevant work done in time will be how early, relative to takeoff, we can automate or uplift it. Most straightforwardly, if by default we would only be able to automate it at or after AI R&amp;D automation, automating or uplifting the work earlier than that would dramatically increase the chance that we can complete the work in time.</span><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eimAN67tiNsrrtnbK/920154e9faa96ab974915010510f28a520584e301e024aeb85bf586b775dc087/bwyd8ns6ebas6qe9orn7" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this diagram, we use "AI safety" to refer broadly to any work to make AI go well, including technical and non-technical interventions. The graph shows the effect of differentially accelerating AI safety capabilities on total (quality-adjusted) AI safety work. The green dot to the right shows the total quantity of unassisted AI safety work. (None of these areas account for the advantage of earlier work. In reality, unassisted human work matters much more than its volume suggests, because it comes earlier.)</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But even if by default we are able to automate the relevant work months before AI R&amp;D automation, doing so earlier is still very valuable. For one, since AI systems might be misaligned and hence untrustworthy</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffhze7dqnhw9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfhze7dqnhw9">[9]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, we might need human verification and discussion, which will take time. For another, implementing solutions also takes time.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Finally, the earlier we can automate the relevant work relative to when AI reaches a given level of misalignment and scheming, the better, because it may be difficult to prevent sandbagging, and work from coherent schemers will obviously be less trustworthy. (We expect conceptual reasoning to accelerate AI safety more than it accelerates scheming; see the previous section.)</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The discussion above assumed that the only relevant "deadline" is takeoff (or a point shortly before it). In reality, however, we will face many decisions that cannot be postponed without cost, such as prioritisation decisions or decisions about which interventions to implement. The quality of each such decision is then determined by the total amount of quality-adjusted work that has gone into answering the questions underlying the decision (including by surfacing considerations not originally motivated by the decision). Many of these nearer "deadlines" will also move with the time of takeoff (e.g., "the penultimate training run before AI R&amp;D automation"), so the timing of a given level of AI safety capabilities relative to takeoff matters here too. For many decisions, the combined AI safety capabilities of humans and AI at the time of the decision also matter. We want AI to help us make the best decisions possible at any given point in time.</span><br /><br /><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note: Some of the above is based on a somewhat broad distribution over how much work is necessary and when the relevant capabilities will arrive. If one thought that only a relatively small amount of AI safety work is necessary (e.g., that human labour alone would be enough) or that the capabilities necessary to automate it will arrive very early (e.g., years before takeoff), earlier AI assistance would be unimportant. We find the first of these views plausible, but not overwhelmingly likely; it would also reduce the value of much other AI safety work. We find the second of these views implausible, based on the relevant capability trends we have observed. At the other extreme, if one thought that it would be completely hopeless to complete the relevant work in time, earlier AI assistance with technical AI safety work would also be unimportant, although AI assistance might still be valuable for uplifting policy work (e.g., if AI can reason better about AI risk, perhaps it can better convince politicians and the public that AI risk is a big problem). We find this latter view plausible, but we don't assign a high enough credence to it to endorse its conclusions.</span></i></li></ol><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eimAN67tiNsrrtnbK/82de32b65897cae1897eccfce5e8ec615bc5ac1db1f19fa4863285beeef3d9c4/i3dfbtmijxo2xooifeiw" /><ol start="2"><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Path dependencies:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Society's views on various topics, including ethics, may be path dependent. For instance, a given human's moral views might depend on which arguments she encounters and when, and once she holds particular views, she may want to take action to make sure future agents have the same moral views as her. AI steering human reflection in more reasonable directions (and reasoning sensibly about what kinds of steering it should and shouldn't do) could therefore have a lasting impact. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(This post is not primarily about path dependence, so we won't argue for its plausibility here. For some discussion of path dependence, including whether anti-path-dependence intuitions should resolve it, see</span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5ro9kSxhP8mHXGmn4/against-strong-decision-theoretic-realism-1"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, e.g., Emery's post on decision-theoretic antirealism</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FSmPtu7foXwNYpWiB/on-the-limits-of-idealized-values"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the limits of idealized values</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and Emery's upcoming post on the time sensitivity of influencing AI's decision theory).</span></i><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We illustrate this with the (oversimplified) diagram below. Consider, for example, society's moral views. We think these have many possible basins of attraction. Despite being moral antirealists, we also think that some of these basins are more reasonable than others. For instance, we expect thoughtful humans to share, at least partially, various intuitions about what a reasonable reflection process would and would not involve. Such reflection processes push towards some basins and away from others. In the figure below, "the winds of reason" represent the force of these reasonable reflection processes. The "great basin" represents one of the kinds of views that these reflection processes push towards. The "sucky basin" represents a basin of views that might instead arise by default, from a more chaotic or misaligned process</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkgalvcibouo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkgalvcibouo">[10]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Making AI better able to help humans reflect on philosophical questions earlier (e.g., relative to superhuman persuasion abilities) is then akin to starting the "winds of reason" earlier. (Of course, one could also do many other things here that might be good.) This path to impact differs in various ways from the one discussed above, including in which capabilities are most useful for it. Caspar's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/auMyz6pjPX5nGLYsL/the-basic-argument-for-making-ai-better-at-moral-philosophy" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">post on making AI better at moral philosophy</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> discusses this kind of path to impact further.</span><br /><br /><br /><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/eimAN67tiNsrrtnbK/d7155849f48bda86995f5f3751e1433ad4066e38f963403c6cd825cdcdb68ee9/celhwfwbyhuetzd3k08k" /></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, we think it would be very good if, at any point from now</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxje4ajmb15k"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxje4ajmb15k">[11]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> onwards, models were better at helping with all the important challenges we listed above.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can probably differentially accelerate conceptual reasoning</span></h1><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Work on conceptual reasoning is likely counterfactual and tractable</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our experiments suggest that it might be possible to make models several months better on our conceptual argumentation benchmark (</span><a href="https://conceptualreasoning.ai/lmca"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LMCA</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) by training them on a different distribution of conceptual argumentation data, using models of roughly the same strength as a reward signal. We previously had similar success (in terms of months of progress) with prompting and scaffolding, although these results are relatively old. These results don't surprise us much: conceptual reasoning is a somewhat specific skill, which AI companies have little incentive to train and which existing training beyond pretraining seems ill-suited to teach. We elaborate on these two points below.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First, AI companies have only weak incentives to train conceptual capabilities. Previously, we argued that AI R&amp;D isn't very conceptual. Because of this, we think that AI companies will probably be able to automate AI R&amp;D, and get deep into the intelligence explosion, using only the conceptual reasoning ability that AI acquires incidentally through other training. Conceptual reasoning ability also seems to have little commercial value. Indeed, commercially valuable skills typically get direct feedback from the world (e.g., a skill that lets one beat the market over short horizons gets feedback in the form of profit), whereas conceptual reasoning is characterised by a lack of such feedback and of existing data.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefiq29w4zwtx"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fniq29w4zwtx">[12]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> AI companies do have some incentives to train conceptual skills (e.g., as discussed above, predicting takeoff is both useful for the AI industry and partly conceptual), but these seem limited enough that AI companies are likely to neglect conceptual reasoning relative to its altruistic importance.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Moreover, training conceptual capabilities is somewhat different from AI companies' usual activities, such that they may not be well placed to do it on their own. Because conceptual tasks are not straightforwardly verifiable, training may require expert human data</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefc7v6ta57ib5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnc7v6ta57ib5">[13]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or rubrics and prompts written by people who are themselves strong at conceptual reasoning.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Second, we expect AI companies' default training to leave AI worse at conceptual reasoning than it could fairly easily be. Pretraining seems like it should teach conceptual reasoning, because some of the pretraining data is conceptual. But posttraining, and RLVR in particular, makes up a growing fraction of training, and we expect this trend to continue. Conceptual tasks seem relatively ill-served by RLVR, because one cannot train on them directly using RLVR, and the tasks that one can train on in this way are a poor proxy for them. Because reasoning training is RL, this difficulty also seems like a problem for inference-time scaling on conceptual tasks. Our </span><a href="https://conceptualreasoning.ai/methodology"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">results on </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LMCA seem to support this picture: models are improving on it over time, presumably mostly because of better pretraining, but scaling inference-time compute via effort levels yields at best weak gains.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One possible objection is that conceptual reasoning might mostly consist of general intelligence plus a few easy skills on top. If so, models might acquire the skills specific to conceptual reasoning relatively early, after which we would just wait for general intelligence to catch up via lots of generic training (e.g., RLing on tons of maths). Work on conceptual reasoning would then not be very counterfactual. We find it plausible that conceptual reasoning, along with most other skills, is in some sense mostly driven by general intelligence, but we don't think the conclusion follows. In particular, we would still need to ensure that models are well elicited on conceptual tasks (and, if they are scheming, that they don't sandbag on them). Even if models have all the underlying skills that conceptual reasoning requires, conceptual tasks look and feel different enough from non-conceptual tasks that models won't necessarily apply those skills without training. Eliciting models well on conceptual tasks in turn requires at least a way to measure progress on them, and we expect targeted work on such measurement to be extremely valuable. (Most of our work focuses on producing benchmarks and similar resources.) Relatedly, we have found that models are quite bad at mathematics embedded in a conceptual context, despite apparently being very good at mathematics posed in isolation. This supports the view that improving conceptual reasoning is partly a matter of elicitation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aside: We don't necessarily expect much generalisation between different conceptual domains. For instance, some AI alignment work seems relatively far from moral philosophy. And empirically, our sense is that generalisation from small-scale training is pretty weak. Our view on this has evolved over time: based on empirical evidence, we have gradually updated to expect less generalisation. For this reason, it's possible that in the future we decide to focus more narrowly on the domains we think are most important. That said, our current view is that it makes sense to think about conceptual reasoning as a whole on methodological grounds: all conceptual domains raise similar fundamental questions about measurement, and some techniques might be applicable to a wide range of conceptual domains.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Effects on generic capabilities are likely small</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Above, we gave reasons to think that AI R&amp;D relies much less on conceptual reasoning than domains such as AI alignment and philosophy do. Here, we turn from the capabilities themselves to work on conceptual reasoning in practice, and give three reasons to expect the effects of such work to be highly differential.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First, the effects of our work on generic capabilities are less likely to be counterfactual. Work on conceptual reasoning surely affects many different capabilities: some useful for the work we want to accelerate, some useful for generic capabilities, some useful for both, and some useful for neither. Of these, the ones that contribute to the generic capabilities AI companies are targeting are disproportionately likely to be redundant with existing training, because AI companies' other training is more likely to produce them anyway. For instance, one might expect some transfer from LMCA (our conceptual argumentation dataset) to mathematics, since there is some overlap in the relevant reasoning styles. But it is much easier to get the relevant skill in mathematics simply by training on problems with verifiable rewards, once pretraining data has been exhausted.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref42oh61v98b1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn42oh61v98b1">[14]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The same seems to hold for ML. Although some ML tasks are too expensive to train directly based on verifiable outcomes (e.g., designing a training plan for the next frontier model from start to finish), many of the relevant skills for these tasks are likely also to feature in less expensive tasks, on which outcome-based RL is feasible. Additionally, to the extent that outcome-based rewards alone aren't the most effective way to train ML research taste, AI companies might, for instance, make datasets of judgements from their top ML researchers (given that the companies' usage of the models for AI R&amp;D plausibly quite naturally leads to the construction of such datasets). If so, any residual effect of other conceptual reasoning work on ML research taste would be much smaller.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Second, we can focus on particular conceptual reasoning domains and avoid those that seem most relevant to timelines. The conceptual skills most relevant to AI R&amp;D probably differ somewhat from those most relevant to the domains we focus on. However, this consideration is weaker than it first appears, for two reasons. Our datasets might enable people to test techniques aimed at a broader set of domains (see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Counterpoint__Using_our_datasets_to_develop_generic_techniques"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">below</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). And by advocating for work on conceptual reasoning, we might lead people who target their work less carefully to work on it. (We did price this in when deciding to write this post!)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Finally, we think that there are good </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a priori </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reasons to expect downsides from accelerating generic capabilities to be (much) less important than our opportunity costs, based on how non-neglected generic capabilities are. For more on this, see Caspar's post on</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><a href="https://casparoesterheld.com/2026/08/25/a-simple-argument-about-capabilities-externalities-versus-opportunity-costs/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a simple argument about capabilities externalities versus opportunity costs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We expect this </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a priori </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">argument to mostly apply here.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Counterpoint: Using our datasets to develop generic techniques</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We mostly view our datasets as valuable because they’re the only good datasets for some set of domains X that we want to accelerate. But there is plausibly a much larger set of domains Y for which one of our datasets is also the only or best dataset. If so, that dataset might enable work on Y. For example, LMCA is probably the best, and perhaps the only good, dataset of rated arguments in general. Possibly it is also the best publicly available</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefiw5kk76l8s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fniw5kk76l8s">[15]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> dataset on “fuzzy tasks” (i.e., tasks without cheaply verifiable ground-truth rewards</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefg65rjhuc53w"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fng65rjhuc53w">[16]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). If so, LMCA might enable work on fuzzy tasks more generally: if Alice has a generic technique for fuzzy tasks (say, debate) that one should expect to work just as well for conceptual argumentation as for other fuzzy tasks (such as reasoning about how to maximise AI companies' revenue), then LMCA would allow her to test it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This concern is hard to assess without a better sense of which generic techniques our dataset will counterfactually enable people to test. But based on conversations with employees of AI companies, we expect that this is a reasonable concern.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Two considerations seem to mitigate this concern somewhat. First, if a capability is important to AI companies, they will likely want to make a benchmark that targets it more directly (although our dataset could still give them at least one relevant benchmark </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">earlier</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Second, testing against our benchmark rather than some other benchmark might steer the development of the relevant technique in a beneficial direction, e.g., towards techniques that work especially well on conceptual tasks.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our work</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To date, we have focused mainly on producing conceptual reasoning datasets and benchmarks, rather than on training or developing techniques. This is largely because better availability of high-quality datasets will unblock other work on this problem: dataset construction requires conceptual reasoning expertise, which is our comparative advantage, and few high-quality conceptual reasoning datasets exist. Moreover, datasets are a prerequisite for almost any other intervention, and enable others (e.g., those who specialise more in ML, or even future AI agents) to contribute. Datasets are also somewhat robustly useful (e.g., they can also be used to predict future developments and aid in prioritisation). You can read more about our datasets, and see results, on our </span><a href="https://conceptualreasoning.ai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conceptual Reasoning Index</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (CRI) website. We're also very interested in including others' high-quality conceptual reasoning datasets in the CRI, so we encourage you to contact us if you have or are working on a dataset that you'd like to see included.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of our current projects is a dataset to assess models' performance at generative conceptual tasks that are exactly on-distribution for what we want models to be good at. Measuring models' performance at generative tasks is challenging, because it requires automated grading, and LLM graders are easily hacked. But this also makes developing better ways to evaluate performance on these tasks especially important.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are also interested in improving propensities related to conceptual reasoning. For instance, we currently think that the poor legibility of models' reasoning limits their usefulness and trustworthiness for conceptual reasoning. Models often write in vague and confusing ways that seem optimised to obscure their mistakes (perhaps because RLAIF rewards this). We think resolving this problem might be extremely important. Additionally,</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MzenSrmZ3pT2pCnvp/frontier-models-state-different-decision-theory-preferences-2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> models' answers to conceptual questions currently seem to be heavily tailored to the user in opaque ways</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This seems bad, although the ideal behaviour here is somewhat up for debate. We are working on a dataset to better measure behaviours like sycophancy on conceptual domains.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We discuss our thoughts on prioritisation within conceptual reasoning in more detail in an upcoming post.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In brief, the domains we currently prioritise most for differential acceleration are technical AI safety, decision theory, </span><a href="https://longtermrisk.org/research/using-surrogate-goals-deflect-threats/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">safe Pareto</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.cs.cmu.edu/~conitzer/safeAAMAS21.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">improvements</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and aspects of cause prioritisation that are particularly conceptual. Improving AI in some other domains, such as forecasting the intelligence explosion, might also be valuable, but carries a higher risk of dual use or other negative externalities.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We're always looking for more people to work on our agenda. If you're interested in working with us, or on something similar, please contact us.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Acknowledgements</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are especially grateful to Alex Kastner, Lukas Finnveden, and Buck Shlegeris for their extensive feedback. We would also like to thank Alexa Pan, Anthony DiGiovanni, Carlo Leonardo Attubato, Carlos Giudice, Eli Lifland, Francis Rhys Ward, Hasan Baig, Jeremy Gillen, Jo Jiao, Julian Stastny, Keshav Shenoy, Lukas Gloor, Megan Kinniment</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Miles Kodama, Nathaniel Sauerberg, Netzer Epstein, Rohin Shah, Thomas Larsen, Vojta Kovarik, and Zephaniah Roe for helpful comments and discussions.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendices</span></h1><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: Examples and non-examples of conceptual domains</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This appendix gives examples to illustrate what we mean by conceptual reasoning. For the domains we currently think are most worth accelerating, see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Our_work"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our work</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ethics. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We view ethics as a prototypical conceptual domain.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4y9zymtosam"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4y9zymtosam">[17]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It clearly meets our criteria: it lacks an established methodology for resolving disagreements; it gets no feedback from the world; and progress in it comes primarily from argumentation.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mathematics.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Mathematics is mostly </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> conceptual by our definition. In particular, assessing whether proposed mathematical statements are true (by proof or counterexample) is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> conceptual, because it is fully verifiable. Answering fully defined questions (say, solving the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basel_problem"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Basel problem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) is similarly not conceptual.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some applied mathematics, such as applied statistics, is essentially grounded in predicting real-world events, so real-world feedback makes it non-conceptual as well.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That said, we would view some foundational activities in mathematics as conceptual: generally, those related to formalising concepts that we have some intuitions about, and choosing which questions to work on (when not guided by real-world or mathematical application). For instance, developing axiomatic foundations of mathematics is conceptual — in particular, reflecting on which axioms are “natural” (e.g., whether to assume the axiom of choice). By contrast, the technical question of whether a given axiomatisation is strong enough to derive all of standard mathematics is non-conceptual. The foundations of logic and probability, and some basic ideas in learning theory (like the concept of regret), are also somewhat conceptual.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Importantly, not all definitional or field-founding work is conceptual. Much of it is driven largely by practical application and real-world feedback. For instance, the basic concepts of linear algebra systematise long-understood ideas with immediate, known practical applications, such as solving systems of linear equations. Similarly, the foundational concepts of computational complexity theory (e.g., worst-case big-O analysis and the classes P and NP) can be viewed as conceptually natural, but their centrality stems mostly from practical considerations. In particular, many practical problems turn out to be NP-complete, which makes NP a natural complexity class to focus on. And although people probably care more about exact runtimes than about worst-case big-O complexity for applications, exact runtime bounds are extremely hard to derive theoretically, whereas big-O analysis is far more tractable while still being informative enough in practice.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Decisions about what to work on can be conceptual or non-conceptual:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In pure mathematics, one sometimes picks questions because they are intrinsically interesting rather than useful for a particular purpose, and investigates theories because they are elegant in some sense. For instance, people sometimes call category theory elegant, citing, e.g., its ability to unify results from different areas of mathematics, while also saying that it is not very useful in some sense. (We’ve similarly heard people say that string theory is elegant but useless.) Such judgements of interestingness and elegance seem fairly conceptual.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In many other cases, decisions about what to work on are driven by practical features on which one gets real-world feedback. For instance, one might work on topics on which one feels able to make progress. Experience probably trains this feeling: one learns from the outcomes of past decisions about what to work on, and from whether one’s past predictions of progress turned out to be correct.</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Research on social choice theory. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a typical example of a discipline that mixes conceptual and non-conceptual work. Some things are very conceptual: choosing and prioritising between desiderata (e.g., the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Condorcet_winner"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Condorcet criterion</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) for voting rules</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefymzwi2znfej"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnymzwi2znfej">[18]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">deciding</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to try to prove or disprove </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrow%27s_impossibility_theorem"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Arrow's impossibility theorem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; choosing modelling assumptions (e.g., whether preferences are cardinal or ordinal). Other things are mostly non-conceptual: proving formal results about voting rules (e.g., </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">proving</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Arrow’s impossibility theorem); running computational experiments to test, for instance, how common incentives to misreport preferences are.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Longtermist macrostrategy. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a somewhat complicated mix, but much of it seems fairly conceptual. The high-level question of what we should do is somewhat conceptual, because we get little feedback on it. Questions such as the following also seem fairly conceptual: how large the risk from deceptively aligned AI is, whether acausal trade makes sense, and whether the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doomsday_argument"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">doomsday argument</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or the Fermi paradox should make us very worried. Questions involving ethics, such as how much to care about </span><a href="https://nickbostrom.com/papers/astronomical-waste/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">astronomical waste</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, are clearly very conceptual. Other questions are more mixed. For instance, predicting the long-run trajectory of a civilisation like ours under different circumstances (e.g., if China became the dominant global superpower, would its values be fine in the long run?) is fairly conceptual, but also informed by various empirical considerations.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The "macro" part here is important: the more we get into micro-level prioritisation considerations, the less conceptual things get.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Persuasion. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We mostly view persuasion as non-conceptual, because one can ultimately learn it better from real-world experience than from a priori reasoning. It also has relatively fast feedback loops and is substantially a matter of human psychology, which one can study empirically.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That said, coming up with good conceptual arguments is conceptual, and it helps with persuasion. On the other hand, the best arguments are often not the most persuasive. For instance, persuasive arguments often appeal to quirks of human psychology, such as loss aversion, whereas good arguments often have to discuss nuances that weaken their case.
