More generally, a better understanding of a conceptual domain helps with persuasion in that domain.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Relatedly, in some sense, one can view every question as asking what we would find persuasive at the end of a long, large-scale, idealised reflection process. Writing arguments that would persuade such a reflection process is therefore conceptual. But we think this is quite a different activity from trying to persuade, say, politicians or members of the public today.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Being a good CEO.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We view this as mostly non-conceptual in ordinary times, because we expect most business decisions to depend mainly on aggregating a lot of practical experience and information (although none of us has personal experience of being a CEO). Being a good CEO in unprecedented times, such as during an intelligence explosion, is different, because being a good futurist might then be an important part of the job. CEOs might also sometimes have to ask themselves ethical questions, which are as conceptual as ethical questions always are.</span></p></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: is ML bottlenecked by conceptual insights?</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this appendix, we consider whether work on conceptual reasoning could accelerate AI timelines by providing conceptual insights that AI R&amp;D would otherwise lack. Specifically, models might be able to learn some abilities from, say, LMCA that they would struggle to learn from AI companies’ default training, and these abilities might be useful for AI R&amp;D. We think this is possible, but we doubt that such insights usually bottleneck AI development.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s a bit difficult to come up with a convincing example of this, but our go-to one is that LMCA is probably good for learning to reason sensibly about whether next-token prediction can </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in principle</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> scale to ASI, whether LLMs will always be merely “stochastic parrots” who can’t have a world model and so on. Training in AI R&amp;D environments, meanwhile, may not help much with this, because concepts like ASI lack a reference class that could be present in training. We think these particular questions are no longer relevant, so making models good at reasoning about them is harmless. In principle, however, future AI R&amp;D might hinge on similar questions about the nature of intelligence, which conceptual reasoning datasets would help models reason about, rather than on more prosaic skills of the kind that, say, predicting the outcomes of experiments teaches well.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This worry is more serious because one can easily tell a story of why AI companies might undervalue AI capabilities related to such insights, without simply appealing to general incompetence. For instance, much of the value of these capabilities may come from low-probability or one-off, high-impact events, and decisions based on such events are much harder to make.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conceptual reasoning might also help with structuring very large quantities of knowledge (say, thousands of papers) and organising very large research fields. In principle, AI could learn this ability entirely from RL in AI R&amp;D environments, but this would seem to require training on extremely long reasoning traces, which is very expensive, even with budgets of hundreds of billions of dollars.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnn17oewvq0s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnn17oewvq0s">[19]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We guess that various conceptual capabilities will help with this. For instance, we think whether a distinction is “principled” in some philosophical sense is probably a decent predictor of whether it will remain useful.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, we doubt that conceptual insights of this kind usually bottleneck AI development. For instance, if all potential confusions about next-token prediction had been resolved in, say, 2019, we think timelines would have shortened only modestly. There are plenty of loss functions that can </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in principle</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> take you to AGI. (E.g., predicting all the world’s camera feeds can also take you to AGI, in principle.) Conceptual reasoning helps with figuring out whether a source of data can get you to AGI in principle. But we think the difficulty was mostly in finding a specific type of data and training algorithm that one can get to scale </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in practice</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and then in correctly guessing based on smaller-scale experiments that this approach would scale quite well. Conceptual reasoning seems close to useless for figuring out which approach works </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in practice</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, whereas training in AI R&amp;D environments is probably great for this.</span></p></div></details><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8wak8jgg4uv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8wak8jgg4uv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Quoting Joe Carlsmith: "For research in some domains, though, our evaluation efforts can’t benefit from strong empirical feedback loops or from formal evaluation methods. Rather, our central tool, for evaluating this kind of research, is … thinking about it. And also: arguing. But not in a way where: after thinking about it, you get to see the answer. Rather: you think about it, you argue, you come to a view, and … that’s it. I’ll call this sort of research “conceptual research.” Paradigm examples, to my mind, include philosophy, futurism, and certain kinds of political debate. To be clear: work in these domains can draw on empirical data, too. And often (for example, in the context of futurism), there is in fact a ground-truth empirical answer (e.g., what will in fact happen in the future). [...] I’m thinking of research as “conceptual” insofar as our evaluation of it can’t benefit from empirical tests/feedback loops prior to the point where we need to evaluate it."</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn16c2u3pqocn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref16c2u3pqocn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Which is itself a conceptual task.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnysjhtqbwj7r"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefysjhtqbwj7r">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Especially for the more mathematical elements of conceptual domains, like whether a proposed ethical theory will be Dutch bookable.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fno9yy68j3u8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefo9yy68j3u8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One might hope that one can cheaply elicit all the relevant human judgements to start with, and be left with a verifiable question. If so, maybe we could just train models to predict MTurkers, and then the rest will be taken care of by training on verifiable domains, like mathematics. If so, these normative questions might not need dedicated work for AI to answer them. It seems like there are a few problems with this, however. First, the questions that one might need to ask humans might not be easy to answer, and might not be easy to understand. (E.g., it seems like asking MTurkers which philosophical bullet they prefer to bite would not be very useful in most cases.) It's not clear if one could practically reduce the questions further. (Often, it seems like when answering a philosophical question, things like values and things like capabilities are densely tangled.) Second, the number of questions one might need the answer to might be extremely large, if one were forced to ask them all in advance. Finally, even if something like this in principle works, we would need to ensure the models are well-elicited. This requires us to be able to evaluate models' ability to answer such questions, which we cannot yet do well.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntebrht53frp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftebrht53frp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Examples from Joe Carlsmith's essay. Note that we haven't checked each of the items and so don't necessarily agree with his classification of works as conceptual:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Early work on scalable oversight techniques, like</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1810.08575"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Christiano et al (2018)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on iterated amplification and distillation; </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1805.00899"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving et al (2018) </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">on AI safety via debate; and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1811.07871"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Leike et al (2018)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on recursive reward modeling.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Efforts like </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2208.08345"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kenton et al (2022)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2109.10996"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Garrabrant et al (2021)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to formalize notions of agency.</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2409.03077"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Christiano et al (2024)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> attempting to formalize the notion of defendability against backdoors.</span></li><li value="4"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1609.03543"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Garrabrant et al (2016)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> attempting to formalize reasoning under logical uncertainty.</span></li><li value="5"><a href="https://www.amazon.com/Superintelligence-Dangers-Strategies-Nick-Bostrom/dp/0198739834"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bostrom (2014)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> giving an early version of the case for concern about the alignment problem.</span></li><li value="6"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1906.01820"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hubinger et al (2019)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> analyzing risks from learned optimization.</span></li><li value="7"><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1WwsnJQstPq91_Yh-Ch2XRL8H_EpsnjrC1dwZXR37PC8/edit?tab=t.0#heading=h.kkaua0hwmp1d"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Christiano et al (2021)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> developing the eliciting latent knowledge agenda.</span></li><li value="8"><a href="https://storage.googleapis.com/deepmind-media/DeepMind.com/Blog/evaluating-potential-cybersecurity-threats-of-advanced-ai/An_Approach_to_Technical_AGI_Safety_Apr_2025.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Shah et al (2025)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on Google DeepMind’s overall approach to AGI safety"</span></li></ul></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncl82x3gh7go"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcl82x3gh7go">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are some (mostly less relevant) components of ML research taste that we </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">would</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> view as conceptual. Some questions are “interesting” or “natural”, and some experimental setups “elegant” or “cool”, in a way that isn’t primarily meant to be predictive in the sense we describe.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fngmttq1u6ovw"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgmttq1u6ovw">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that this does rely on it being feasible to run relevant ML experiments. If we lived in the 1950s, ML research would be much more conceptual, because this ground-truth feedback would be unavailable.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnyioqfwmwwgl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyioqfwmwwgl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course, you might think that this is a huge mistake. But if ML researchers who are unusually strong in CR are also unusually good at ML research, we would expect to find that top ML researchers are unusually strong in CR (beyond what would be predicted by their general intelligence, say), and would likely be relatively interested in conceptual ways of thinking (because interest in engaging in a skill predicts strength at that skill). We think labs would probably notice this. (And our very limited info suggests that top ML researchers are not particularly interested in conceptual ways of reasoning, especially compared to top safety researchers.)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfhze7dqnhw9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffhze7dqnhw9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We think that AI labor is still valuable in this case. For instance, it is often much easier to verify a solution to a problem than to produce one. Thus, even if verification is entirely reliant on human labor, using AIs to generate solutions or ideas might speed up research dramatically. Moreover, the threat of being caught and thereby revealing misalignment limits how much the AI can sandbag or sabotage.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnkgalvcibouo"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefkgalvcibouo">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">E.g., consider reflection processes that are driven more by market forces, or by agents leveraging persuasion tools to their own ends.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxje4ajmb15k"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxje4ajmb15k">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why is now already valuable? We think that models are already beginning to be useful for some parts of partially conceptual AI safety work, so that improvements on their current capabilities will make a difference, even if automation is still a long way away. We also think that models are already beginning to play a role in influencing potentially path dependent views (of humans and AI systems).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fniq29w4zwtx"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefiq29w4zwtx">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Relatedly, even customers who value good conceptual reasoning will find it hard to tell which model reasons best, because conceptual reasoning is hard to verify. This further weakens AI companies' commercial incentive to improve it.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnc7v6ta57ib5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefc7v6ta57ib5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And we would guess that human data companies like Mercor, Surge AI, Appen etc. aren't well-placed to provide such data.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn42oh61v98b1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref42oh61v98b1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While RL is often inefficient, especially at longer time horizons, paying humans to generate new data seems much more expensive, and has more fundamental limits (especially as capabilities approach human expert level).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fniw5kk76l8s"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefiw5kk76l8s">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Perhaps the frontier labs have more useful non-public datasets on fuzzy tasks, meanwhile.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fng65rjhuc53w"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefg65rjhuc53w">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that this is broader than conceptual tasks, since it includes tasks that are significantly about applying empirical knowledge. For instance, making a UI design that users will like is a fuzzy task (since getting user input is expensive), but not a conceptual task.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4y9zymtosam"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4y9zymtosam">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For what it’s worth, we’re all moral anti-realists. If you believe morality means obeying God’s commands and that, say, the Bible is the word of God, then morality becomes less conceptual, though interpreting the Bible is still pretty conceptual, due to its imprecision.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnymzwi2znfej"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefymzwi2znfej">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sometimes this can become non-conceptual. E.g., let’s say people want to reform the voting system of a large country. And then one component of designing a new voting system will be getting desiderata from politicians or the electorate. Figuring out what kinds of voting rules will be compelling to people and what voting rules they can even understand is not conceptual.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnn17oewvq0s"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnn17oewvq0s">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can learn a lot of this from organizing existing knowledge as well, though, e.g., as part of literature review. For instance, you might have AI R&amp;D RL environments in which the model can first review the literature and then run some training.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One argument against generalization from literature review is that the literature found in literature review usually comes with some structure / organization. Another argument is that if you read a lot of literature but then only run a single training run, you probably don’t need to do very sophisticated organizing.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eimAN67tiNsrrtnbK/a-basic-case-for-differentially-accelerating-conceptual#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Hair Thickness of My Friends](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/q8gnBtqijGsdqWJ5j/hair-thickness-of-my-friends)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Intro</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have a microscope. It can see hair. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Humans have hair. Some of my friends are humans. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How thick is my friends' hair?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apparently, if I go around asking my friends for a strand of their hair, about half will just give one to me, and almost all the rest will give one when I explain that I'm going to see how thick it is under a microscope. Your mileage may vary. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My parents have not yet chanced upon the card with taped and labelled hair strands, but if they do then they might have some, uh, pointed questions. I'll just tell them I'm trying to diversify my clone army.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hey, at least it's not like that time a friend of a friend's ex once drew someone's blood at a party.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjnyup05sg9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjnyup05sg9">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Unfortunately, I never got around to asking her for one of the blood vials she was known to possess.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Methods</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To measure length, I broke off a tiny piece of a clear ruler and put it under the microscope with the hair. Then, I had Free Claude (from 2025?) write some code so I could compare two lengths easily by clicking points. Then I modified some Claude analysis code because I was too lazy to write it myself. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's what mine looked like (At the time I had some black hair and some bleached bronze-ish hair, and both seemed about as thick, so I just used the latter):</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792984376/lexical_client_uploads/lkp11h5v0rldngs1dwg2.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The reference is 1 mm, so my hair was about 85 microns thick.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Results</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's an anonymized bar plot, where my id is 16. Id 5 and id 6 come from the same person, but the 6th was a sample of hair dyed red and the 5th was of black hair. The colors are according to biological sex (most are trans women because friendship selection pressures):</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792988032/lexical_client_uploads/lyjnjo4amvcyagsnse9n.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The mean's 78 microns and the (</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessel%27s_correction" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bessel corrected</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) sd's 12. That's in line with </span><a href="https://link.springer.com/content/pdf/10.1684/ejd.2015.2726.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this international study</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefr4f46j3powr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnr4f46j3powr">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (I live in America and most participants were some flavor of white):</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792990285/lexical_client_uploads/k07hrlltnpk1wbnkwgml.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And look, 5/16 or about 31% of my </span><s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">victims</span></s><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> subjects are outside of one sd of the mean, and 1/16 or about 6% got quite close to two sds. </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/68%E2%80%9395%E2%80%9399.7_rule" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I guess the statisticians weren't lying after all</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Male group got </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, female group got </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, doing a (</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welch%27s_t-test" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Welch</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) t-test for the difference. The difference of 1.3 microns has a Welch's t statistic of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> degrees of freedom, which gives a large 95% CI of about </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and a p-value of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Personally I suspect some assumption is broken that makes the CI quite a bit tighter. Regardless, they have basically the same mean.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let's keep trying to make a mountain out of a molehill of data and test the male variability hypothesis, using a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown%E2%80%93Forsythe_test" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Brown-Forsythe test</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The W statistic is 0.735 and p = 0.4. Running some magical bootstrap code that Claude gives me spits out a 95% CI for the sd ratio of [0.661, 5.391]. Wow, doesn't this nothingburger taste like nothing?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's the empirical distribution function in blue, compared to a normal distribution in green, and confidence bars from some </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dvoretzky%E2%80%93Kiefer%E2%80%93Wolfowitz_inequality" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">magic frequentist statistic gadget</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that I didn't end up caring about because clearly it wasn't saying anything interesting.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792988105/lexical_client_uploads/hihx0ejjq3rvwofnm8ne.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now let's eyeball the result of a related frequentist gadget! To do a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kolmogorov%E2%80%93Smirnov_test#Kolmogorov_distribution" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kolmogorov-Smirnov test</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, we take the max difference between those two curves, multiply by the square of the sample size, and then we'll get something that in the limit looks like this magic distribution:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792985545/lexical_client_uploads/xuwcjosqd90ornk2tdva.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(there's a magic correction</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjjrbz98ol9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjjrbz98ol9">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to speed up the convergence that I'll actually be using). </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The max difference looks to be at about the part at 82% on the x-axis, and the difference looks to be about 15 percentage points. So sqrt(16) * .15 = 4 * .15 = .6, magic calculation says that the area under the curve (adjusted by magic to become .6354) is 0.186, which means that if we truly had a normal distribution there's about an 19% chance we'd get something smaller than this, which is small, which is (</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">checks notes</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) good.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The person with the thickest hair said something like "That's good, I think?". The person with the thinnest hair happens to be pretty shy, so she was probably embarrassed.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A study with a larger sample could try getting actual results for the sex differences, could try comparing bleached vs unbleached hair, dyed vs natural, natural hair color, and region of the head/body. A study with a smaller sample would be faster to conduct. A study with an imaginary sample would help you test your statistical methods and nothing else. The generalization to quaternionic sample sizes is left as an exercise.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For some reason everyone asks whether I collected pubic hair. I can neither confirm nor deny that pubic photons have reached my retinas. I will gladly look at your stats if you want to know how they compare.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ceterum autem censeo frequentistae esse delendam.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjnyup05sg9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjnyup05sg9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This led the host to institute the first party rule: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">do not draw blood</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Don't worry, it was consensual... though the drawee seemed drunk.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thus the principle that if </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> am the voice of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">caution</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, you should run.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnr4f46j3powr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefr4f46j3powr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the table they sum up the sample size among all ethnic groups, and then for some dumb reason they also sum up the... hair diameters... and color tones... and curliness...</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjjrbz98ol9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjjrbz98ol9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Using the one given by Wikipedia, where you replace </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">with </span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">which has a max error of like .2%.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/q8gnBtqijGsdqWJ5j/hair-thickness-of-my-friends#comments">Discuss</a>

### [A cat-belling problem for FDT](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/drteSvhc4fX3Tu9y7/a-cat-belling-problem-for-fdt)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><em>[AI disclosure: I used Opus 5.5 for light edits after my first draft]</em></p>
<p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1710.05060">Functional decision theory</a> (Yudkowsky and Soares 2017) proposes choosing actions as follows (equation 4 in the paper):</p>
<p></p>
<p>Here  is the agent's probability distribution,  is a graph saying how changing a logical variable affects other variables,  is the observation history, and  is utility. The underlines denote dequoting:  is a variable in the agent's model representing the output of the FDT algorithm on these inputs.</p>
<p>In this post, by FDT I mean this specific proposal, not TDT, UDT, or logical EDT. FDT, admittedly, relies on a  operator applied to a logical statement, which is on shaky philosophical foundations:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>Equation (4) is sufficient for present purposes, though it rests on shakier philosophical foundations than (3). Pearl (2000) has given a compelling philosophical account of how to deduce the structure of causation from observation and experiment, but no such formal treatment has yet been given to the problem of deducing the structure of other kinds of subjunctive dependence. Equation (4) works given a graph that accurately describes how changing the value of a logical variable affects other variables, but it is not yet clear how to construct such a thing—nor even whether it can be done in a satisfactory manner within Pearl’s framework. Figuring out how to deduce the structure of subjunctive dependencies from observation and experiment is perhaps the largest open problem in the study of functional decision theory.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>The paper itself recognizes the problem of deducing the structure of logical subjunctive dependence (aka logical counterfactuals) as an open and difficult problem, although elsewhere (Section 3) it describes the difficulty as "technical rather than philosophical". This post argues that the problem is both difficult and philosophical. My own opinion goes further: this is not a well-posed problem, "computational logical counterfactuals" (which I will explain below) do not exist, and FDT needs computational logical counterfactuals, so FDT is ill-defined and cannot be adequately formalized.  However, before defending my stronger opinion, it helps to argue disjunctively that the problem is difficult, impossible, or ill-posed. Although the FDT paper admits to this disjunction, it would help to make the point sharper.</p>
<p>(I previously critiqued logical counterfactuals <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yBdDXXmLYejrcPPv2/two-alternatives-to-logical-counterfactuals">in 2020</a>; Wei Dai <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QPhY8Nb7gtT5wvoPH/comparison-of-decision-theories-with-a-focus-on-logical?commentId=Q4xDaQ79RGED2sLvX">briefly criticized logical counterfactuals in 2019</a>).</p>
<h2>Computational logical counterfactuals</h2>
<p>First, what is a computational logical counterfactual? I assume we are working in a system that can reason about Turing machines, such as PA or ZFC. Call our basic mathematical theory . For now, I assume  is first-order. In the language of , we can encode statements about the outputs of Turing machines. I will use the following notation:</p>
<ul>
<li> states that machine  halts on input , where  is a natural number or finite string.</li>
<li> states that machine , on input , halts and returns , where  is a natural number or finite string.</li>
</ul>
<p>Statements like these are common in Gödelian analysis of PA-like first-order theories. Natural numbers can encode finite strings and vice versa; I omit the details. I assume  is powerful enough to "prove statements about Turing machines" in the following sense:</p>
<ol>
<li>If  is true, then . (Here,  and  are constant natural numbers.)</li>
<li>If  is true, then . (Here, , , and  are constant natural numbers.)</li>
<li> is consistent.</li>
<li>.</li>
<li>.</li>
</ol>
<p>PA satisfies these conditions (at least according to ZFC, which proves PA consistent; conditions 1 and 2 follow from PA's -completeness). I say a <em>computational logical counterfactual</em> is one of the following:</p>
<ul>
<li>, in some case where in fact .</li>
<li>, in some case where in fact .</li>
<li>, in some case where in fact  and .</li>
<li>, where  implies one of the above, both provably in  and "obviously".</li>
</ul>
<p>(Grammatically, a "logical counterfactual" here is a probability evaluation if it were the case that some (actually true) mathematical statement were false. "Logical counterfactual" could more generally in philosophy be taken to mean statements like "if PA were inconsistent, then PA could prove its inconsistency", but FDT uses a probabilistic version, so this is the notion of logical counterfactual I will analyze.)</p>
<p>Of course, I haven't formalized the notion of "obviously". I could say that a <em>counter- logical counterfactual</em> is one of the form  where . Then computational logical counterfactuals are a subset of counter- logical counterfactuals: those which are on statements that pretty clearly imply (falsely, though not necessarily obviously falsely) that some machine does not halt, or does not return some particular result. (By conditions 1, 2, and 4, each of the first three cases is counter-, and so is anything that -provably implies one of them.) Roughly, an implication  is obvious when a human mathematician using GPT-6-Astra-Pro could, without much difficulty, show that .</p>
<h2>FDT needs computational logical counterfactuals</h2>
<p>First, I will argue that FDT needs computational logical counterfactuals. Recall that FDT takes an expectation under . The intention is that FDT is an algorithm:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>Furthermore, FDT agents do not need to evaluate counterpossibilities in full generality; they only need to reason about questions like “How would this predictor’s prediction of my action change if the FDT algorithm had a different output?”</p>
</blockquote>
<p>The paper also implies that such algorithms are computable, rather than e.g. using halting oracles:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>When a prisoner following FDT imagines the world in which she defects, she imagines a world in which the function she is computing outputs defect; and since her twin is computing the same decision function, she assumes the twin will also defect in that world.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Therefore, if there is a working FDT algorithm at all, it is reasonable to say that  can be written as , where  is a Turing machine and  is a finite string encoding the inputs .<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-QeF4zBcSQS2aRLAPS-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-QeF4zBcSQS2aRLAPS-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span> I claim this "can be written as" step is obvious as long as the FDT algorithm is specified.</p>
<p>Let's evaluate whether  is a computational logical counterfactual. First, suppose . Our rules give no clear way to classify this as a computational logical counterfactual, so say it's not. Next, suppose . Then this isn't even a counterfactual, so it's not a computational logical counterfactual. Finally, suppose  for some . Then, by our definition, this is a computational logical counterfactual.</p>
<p>Recall that  is a Turing machine for the FDT algorithm. If  does not halt on , we have a problem: the "algorithm" outputs no action in this situation. This would be a serious flaw in the FDT algorithm. Otherwise, ; in particular,  for some . The FDT algorithm evaluates -interventions on each of its possible actions, iterating over them. In the non-trivial case, that includes both  and actions other than . Evaluating  involves no counterfactual at all. But evaluating  for  involves a computational logical counterfactual.</p>
<p>I hope I have shown that FDT really needs computational logical counterfactuals. The alternatives seem to be that (a) the FDT algorithm fails to halt in some situations, or (b) only one action is available, making the problem trivial. To emphasize: any specification of FDT must specify computational logical counterfactuals. If computational logical counterfactuals are unspecifiable, then so is FDT.</p>
<h2>A cat-belling problem</h2>
<p>I propose that specifying computational logical counterfactuals is a <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SwYBLQvo8MddDcCwz/cat-belling-problems">cat-belling</a> problem for specifying FDT: a step we have some reason to believe is both (a) required for specifying FDT and (b) difficult, if not impossible.</p>
<p>An outside-view argument for difficulty: the FDT paper dates to 2017, and the <a href="https://intelligence.org/files/TDT.pdf">Timeless Decision Theory paper</a> (Yudkowsky 2010) discusses "counterfactual worlds where a deterministic computation returns an output it does not actually return" (a computational logical counterfactual in my sense) as an important input to decision theory, claiming that "Any agent’s ability to make a decision, and the specific decision made, is determined by the agent’s ability to visualize logically impossible counterfactuals". So it's been at least 16 years, and no one has written down a definition of computational logical counterfactuals, despite the Yudkowskian decision theory tradition claiming that they are a critical ingredient for decision theory. On the outside view, that sure indicates difficulty.</p>
<p>If, as I believe, computational logical counterfactuals are ill-posed, that would explain why efforts to specify them have failed. But I don't want to be overconfident; perhaps it is possible to specify them, and it has just been hard so far. I'll now give inside-view arguments for difficulty.</p>
<h2>Inside view: which axiom breaks?</h2>
<p>First, note that a computational logical counterfactual might, in principle, be probabilistic; the FDT algorithm takes expectations under -interventions. This comes up in Pearlian Bayes nets, where an intervention on a node need not lead to a single joint assignment of values to variables, but may instead produce a distribution. Still, Pearlian Bayes nets allow "sampling" a joint assignment from that distribution, using some external source of randomness. I assume computational logical counterfactuals are similar: it's possible to sample a joint assignment of truth values to logical sentences in a counterfactual.</p>
<p>So let's say we sample one or more "logical counterfactual worlds". This sampling need not happen all at once; it could involve lazy evaluation. For example, if we ask whether the trillionth digit of  is 5 in this world, we may need to wait a long time (and keep sampling random bits) for the algorithm to tell us. What we ask of this sample is the following: are all of 's axioms true in the sample?</p>
<p>Of course, it could be that 's axioms all hold with some non-negligible probability and fail with some non-negligible probability. The problem with all of 's axioms being true is the following. If in fact , then  (condition 2). By the soundness theorem,  holds in every model of . (This goes for first-order models of , but more importantly for now, it also goes for complete consistent extensions of , which contain every theorem of .) So if the sample satisfies all of 's axioms and also respects the rules of classical first-order logic, it satisfies , and therefore (by condition 4) does not satisfy  for . But satisfying  was the point of the computational logical counterfactual.</p>
<p>So, for computational logical counterfactuals to be sensible, the samples each need to violate <em>some</em> axiom(s) of . Which axiom is violated can differ between samples, but each sample needs to violate an axiom of .</p>
<p>Say  is PA. Now, which axioms of PA would you be happy to see violated in logical counterfactuals? They all look pretty common-sensical. The induction schema is the most suspicious one. <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robinson_arithmetic">Robinson arithmetic</a> (denoted ) is essentially PA without induction.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-QeF4zBcSQS2aRLAPS-2">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-QeF4zBcSQS2aRLAPS-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span> Is this promising for computational logical counterfactuals?</p>
<p>The problem is that when , we have . The basic reason is that showing a Turing machine returns a specific value, given that it does, only requires checking a finite computation trace, which comes down to a finite sequence of additions and multiplications. There is no need to invoke induction. (Technically:  is -complete; see Smith, <em>An Introduction to Gödel's Theorems</em>, "Q is -complete".) Relatedly,  is strong enough for Gödel's first incompleteness theorem (<a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/goedel-incompleteness/#AriThe">Raatikainen, SEP §1.2.1</a>; Tarski, Mostowski, and Robinson 1953). So even  is too strong for computational logical counterfactuals.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-QeF4zBcSQS2aRLAPS-3">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-QeF4zBcSQS2aRLAPS-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>But none of 's axioms look like good candidates to mess with; they do things like define addition and multiplication. Neither do the axioms of first-order classical logic, which specify the meanings of logical connectives and quantifiers. So it looks like samples of computational logical counterfactuals need to almost always violate some axiom of  (or of first-order classical logic), but there is no good place to posit the violation.</p>
<p>This is the basic inside-view argument for difficulty. If computational logical counterfactuals can be sampled from, their samples must (almost always) violate  or first-order classical logic. There is no obvious way either to reject the possibility of sampling, or to decide which axioms of  (or of first-order classical logic) are worth violating. Any violation could lead to all sorts of crazy consequences, perhaps with corresponding <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hpAbfXtqYC2BrpeiC">Troll Bridge</a> problems; say, the troll blows you up if any axiom of  or of first-order logic is violated.</p>
<h2>Proposals that don't bell the cat</h2>
<p>Now I will address various ways people might claim that logical counterfactuals exist and are feasible to specify.</p>
<p><strong>Logical conditionals.</strong> People may mentally substitute "logical conditional" (i.e. subjective probability conditional on some logical statement, under some mechanism for logical uncertainty) for "logical counterfactual", and then point to something like <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1609.03543">Logical Induction</a> (Garrabrant et al. 2016) for logical conditionals. Logical Induction does show it is possible to reason theoretically about what computationally bounded agents might believe about logical statements. Yet these are conditionals, not counterfactuals. They may work for specifying the evidential variant of FDT sketched in footnote 9 of the FDT paper, but not FDT itself.</p>
<p><strong>Model-theoretic counterfactuals.</strong> People may argue that (non-computational) counterfactuals on mathematical facts are well-defined. This might be true: PA is very likely consistent, and yet (if so) PA +  is also consistent, by Gödel's second incompleteness theorem, making it possible to reason from a false mathematical premise. Still, since , this is not a counter-PA counterfactual, let alone a computational logical counterfactual. (Note that  is equivalent to , for a machine  that searches for a PA-proof of . It makes a non-halting machine halt, which my definition does not count.) A secondary, but probably less important, point is that this might give conditionals rather than counterfactuals: perhaps we can model the beliefs of an agent who is uncertain about  and who reasons conditional on .</p>
<p><strong>Physical counterfactuals.</strong> People may imagine a physical computer running. Partway through, a cosmic ray hits the computer and its state changes, so a different computer state results. It is as if the same original program returned a different value. But this is not a computational logical counterfactual. It is a physical counterfactual on a physical state change to the computer, and the change means the physical computer no longer properly implements the original program. If used for decision theory, this might yield something CDT-like.</p>
<p>I hope it is clear from the above why I think these proposals fail to bell the cat of computational logical counterfactuals. If in fact , these proposals say nothing useful about  for .</p>
<h2>Conclusion</h2>
<p>For FDT to be well-specified, computational logical counterfactuals need to be well-specified, at least for the case of counterfactuals on the FDT algorithm returning different values. Furthermore, specification is essential to FDT, since FDT needs to counterfact on <em>some</em> algorithm returning a different value, and every particular algorithm is specific. Computational logical counterfactuals are difficult-to-impossible to specify, based both on outside-view considerations, and inside-view considerations. I propose that computational logical counterfactuals are a cat-belling problem for FDT, since FDT needs them, while they are difficult; and it's common for attempts at defining "logical counterfactuals" or FDT to glance off this critical problem.</p>
<h2>References</h2>
<ul>
<li>Garrabrant, S., Benson-Tilsen, T., Critch, A., Soares, N., and Taylor, J. (2016). <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1609.03543">Logical Induction</a>. arXiv:1609.03543.</li>
<li>Demski, A. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hpAbfXtqYC2BrpeiC">Troll Bridge</a>. LessWrong.</li>
<li>Pearl, J. (2000). <em>Causality: Models, Reasoning, and Inference</em>. Cambridge University Press.</li>
<li>Raatikainen, P. <a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/goedel-incompleteness/"><em>Gödel’s Incompleteness Theorems</em></a>. Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy.</li>
<li>Smith, P. (2013). <em>An Introduction to Gödel’s Theorems, 2nd ed</em>. Cambridge University Press.</li>
<li>Tarski, A., Mostowski, A., and Robinson, R. M. (1953). <em>Undecidable Theories</em>. North-Holland.</li>
<li>Yudkowsky, E. (2010). <a href="https://intelligence.org/files/TDT.pdf">Timeless Decision Theory</a>. Machine Intelligence Research Institute.</li>
<li>Yudkowsky, E. and Soares, N. (2017). <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1710.05060">Functional Decision Theory: A New Theory of Instrumental Rationality</a>. arXiv:1710.05060.</li>
</ul>
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-QeF4zBcSQS2aRLAPS-1"><p>This assumes  and  have finite representations, as they must if they are inputs to an algorithm. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-QeF4zBcSQS2aRLAPS-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-QeF4zBcSQS2aRLAPS-2"><p>More precisely,  drops the induction schema and adds the single axiom that every nonzero number is a successor, which PA proves by induction. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-QeF4zBcSQS2aRLAPS-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-QeF4zBcSQS2aRLAPS-3"><p>Whether  also proves uniqueness of outputs (condition 4) for specific  and  depends on details of the arithmetization. But this doesn't help: any sample satisfying  still satisfies , along with every other true halting fact, such as the output of a predictor's simulation of . Such a sample is not a world in which  returns  <em>instead of</em> , and nothing downstream of 's output changes. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-QeF4zBcSQS2aRLAPS-3">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
