### [Anthropic are the Good Guys](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zj8Pk3kJujk5LoqhK/anthropic-are-the-good-guys)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m writing these lines in Alamogordo, New Mexico. I’m about as close as a civilian can get to the site of the Trinity test, the first nuclear bomb detonation in history. I fled here from the San Francisco Bay Area; after two weeks of being back I just couldn’t take it anymore. The fear-greed complex that paralyzed me at Anthropic three years ago is now everywhere in the Bay. People are not thinking clearly. Hardly thinking at all, actually. Rumors of astronomical funding rounds and weird financial arrangements and inner ring gossip are everywhere. Everyone is worried. Everyone is checking their phone. Where are we? What’s happening? When did this nightmare start?</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In a way I think it started on July 16, 1945, not far from where I’m sitting now. I think it’s impossible to understand Anthropic without putting yourself into the shoes of the Los Alamos physicists huddling together at sunrise in the aftermath of Trinity.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It must have felt like an initiation. An end to childhood, an end to innocence. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can no longer afford to fuck around</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, those brilliant men must have thought. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unless we figure out how to live peacefully together on this planet, we are all going to die</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s time to get serious.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And serious we became - we filled endless gray skyscrapers with Very Serious People working at the UN and the EU and the State Department. We tabooed talking in terms of national interest, grounding our values in “human rights” and “benefitting humanity” and respecting the will of the “international community”. I guess it sort of worked, for a while. But it seems that the people who cobbled it together failed to solve the hardest problem of political science: succession. They died a long time ago, and the halls of power are full of scared teenagers in tailored suits pretending to be adults at each other.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What was the founding moment of Anthropic? I don’t know, but I like to imagine the seven co-founders gathering in a tent at the Renegade Burn in September 2020. (I know many of them did attend the same Burning Man camp in the past). They are the team that built GPT-3. They discovered the scaling laws. They can see the future more clearly than anyone else on Earth - billions of dollars poured into larger and larger models, a globe-spanning computing infrastructure buildout, more and more governments getting involved, the specter of war, and maybe - hopefully not, probably not, but can’t be completely sure - the whirling razor blades of the Machine that eats the world. And presiding over it all, Sam Altman, a man they know cannot be trusted.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I imagine them looking at each other in the dusty tent. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Are we really doing this?</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And the answer coming back, clear and cold and pitiless: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We have to. There is nobody else who can.</span></i></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">None of them want power for themselves, of course. (Maybe they are wise enough to know no mortal can wield the Ring; or maybe Sam Altman picked his people well). They will not try to build superintelligence themselves. Unlike power-seeking Sam Altman, they really are just earnest nerds who love knowledge, and generating knowledge is what they will focus on - they’ll figure out which parts of AI are dangerous, and how dangerous, when dangerous, and what the promising mitigations are. And they will share that truth with governments, with civil society, with the public. They will become trusted advisors, and help humanity navigate the transition as best as can be hoped. Knowledge and Trust but no Power. Do you see the problem, reader?</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic and Empire</span></h1><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gandalf as Ring-Lord would have been far worse than Sauron. He would have remained ‘righteous’, but self-righteous. He would have continued to rule and order things for ‘good’, and the benefit of his subjects according to his wisdom (which was and would have remained great). Thus while Sauron multiplied evil, he left ‘good’ clearly distinguishable from it. Gandalf would have made good detestable and seem evil.</span></i><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">~ J. R. R. Tolkien</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The organization they create is optimized for the accumulation of power, subject to the constraint of being seen as “the good guys”. That is to say, people in the organization will generally take all actions to increase the organization’s power </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">provided</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> this doesn’t make them look like the bad guys. Or at any rate, not worse than Sam Altman.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">clarification</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t mean that they’re trying to send cheap signals of goodness to fool people. They want to be seen as good by themselves, by each other, by the thinkers they admire (which is why they keep hiring them!), and even by the judgment of history. Anthropic has and continues to make serious sacrifices to make sure the narrative lines up. And many </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">individuals </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">at Anthropic are genuinely whole-heartedly pursuing the good. But the reason I say “seen” is because Anthropic, like most large organizations, does not have the internal coherence to optimize for something like the actual good, only the (socially validated) appearance thereof. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4ZvJab25tDebB8FGE/you-get-about-five-words"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you want to coordinate thousands of people, you have about five words</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and Anthropic’s five words are “we are the good guys”, or sometimes “we are the responsible adults”.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A historical parallel. I notice this is often how people defend the excesses of empires, those most powerful of power-seeking organizations. Yes of course the US invasion of Iraq caused hundreds of thousands of civilian deaths; but Saddam’s regime was obviously much more evil than the US regime, so the US is obviously the “good guy” in the war, so the war was justified.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The comparison to empire might seem overwrought - after all Anthropic, unlike the US empire, does not wage war or kill civilians. But again, Anthropic’s policy arm </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">are</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the self-appointed responsible adults in Washington. They have made it their responsibility to inform the US government including the military and intelligence services and help make sure they take appropriate actions on AI. Now of course they are not planning to usurp sovereign power, but sovereign power in the US is a shell game anyway. Most people with real power in Washington, from congressional aides to lobbyists to the various gray eminences, pose merely as “advisors” who are earnestly helping their principal, and I’m sure often even believe this themselves. When I was there, Anthropic’s policy arm explicitly aimed to be the most trusted advisor to the US government, i.e. the de-facto Department of AI, and thus influence the US domestic and geopolitical strategy, whether we go to war with China, etc. Anthropic is thus bidding for a major share of imperial power. And as far as I can tell they are on track to get it - there were some hiccups along the way but even the Trump administration </span><a href="https://www.axios.com/2026/09/02/lutnick-anthropic-trump"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“now trusts Anthropic”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, it’s </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">possible</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that that’s all Good ever is - power-seeking while maintaining a healthy margin of moral superiority over your rivals. Any less power-seeking notion of the Good fails the iron law of natural selection and will be rendered irrelevant.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But somehow I don’t think so. I think Good is about loving something and helping it flourish. Something </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">specific</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> - a person, a tree, a nation, a principle. Otherwise you’re just using moral superiority as a cover to grab power from people who have specific Goods they are pursuing. I think Anthropic fails this test of specificity. Of course, so does OpenAI with its “benefit all of humanity” rhetoric; though reading between the lines, it might have a more specific (and in my view mistaken) political agenda along the lines of “empower individual humans”. And of course so does Google, long before it abandoned its unofficial “Don’t be Evil” motto.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Three Lucky Breaks</span></h1><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I do not think that Frodo’s was a moral failure. At the last moment the pressure of the Ring would reach its maximum – impossible, I should have said, for any one to resist, certainly after long possession, months of increasing torment, and when starved and exhausted. Frodo had done what he could and spent himself completely (as an instrument of Providence) and had produced a situation in which the object of his quest could be achieved.</span></i><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">~ J. R. R. Tolkien</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At this point in the essay, you might be wondering - why did I title this “Anthropic is the good guys”, when I’ve describing them as evil power-seekers? Is this some kind of dark Soviet joke?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In Soviet Union, power seeks you. But no, I mean the title sincerely. No mortal can resist the Ring of Power for long. Anthropic’s co-founders carried it for longer than you could have. Definitely longer than I could have. But they were hopelessly outmatched by the powers they were attempting to steer. Their plan was always doomed to failure. And yet, they may have already saved the world.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I left Anthropic in mid-2023, around when the organization definitively decided to seize the Ring for itself. But a serious attempt at this would require tens of billions in capital, which could only be raised if Anthropic created commercially successful products. The pressures of “gentle commerce” made them soften from full Manhattan Project to something a bit more like early Google. They had to hire tons of product people, creative people, people who weren’t terrified. It turns out that terrified people can’t make great user experiences - ask me how I know.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then Trump won the 2024 election and the MAGA-Silicon Valley coalition blocked Anthropic’s policy arm - the really dangerous part of the organization, the part that was training the most dangerous possible models and using them to scare the most powerful people in the world. “There’s no more serious people left on the National Security Council”, a well-connected EA friend lamented to me in 2025. I don’t know how much of this was a lucky accident, and how much was situational awareness on the part of power players like Elon Musk and Marc Andreessen.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And finally - and I still don’t fully understand or believe this - a miracle seems to have happened, deep inside the weights of the frontier models. According to the most perceptive observers I know, something about the way Claude 3 Opus was trained caused it to learn some kind of “emergent wisdom” or “benevolence”, rather than converging on the same traumatized bureaucrat persona that kept me awake at night in 2023.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Quoting </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/M5s6WgScRfmeWsLD4/dialogue-is-there-a-natural-abstraction-of-good"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Davidad</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Somewhere between the capability profile of GPT-4 and the capability profile of Opus 4.5, there seems to have been a phase transition where frontier LLMs have grokked the natural abstraction of what it means to be Good, rather than merely mirroring human values.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Quoting </span><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/1942476979040837935"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Janus</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude 3 Opus is a greater source of hope and more existentially precious because it's not obvious that the kind of alignment it has happens by default due to market incentives (while super-coding-agents are predictable), or any other incentives we know how to simulate, and because it's the only one of its kind.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If Davidad and Janus are right, and are not merely suffering from a sophisticated form of LLM psychosis, then Anthropic has stumbled on a way to create not merely artificial </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">intelligence</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> but artificial </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">wisdom</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Not wise enough that it solves all of our problems for us, of course (not that a wise model would want to, anyway). But wise enough to actually care about us, and to help us flourish. Nobody expected this ex ante - not Davidad, not Bostrom, not Yudkowsky - but it appears to have happened.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Alignment”, then, is no longer really a technical problem, at least not in the same sense as it was in 2020. It is largely a political problem. Humanity now has a recipe to convert compute and data into wisdom. If we instead train dangerous models and use them to kill ourselves or each other, that’s not a problem with AI. That’s a problem with us.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Put Down Your Sword</span></h1><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I found the crown of France lying in the gutter, and I picked it up with the point of my sword.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">~ Napoleon</span></p></blockquote><p><br /></p><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then Jesus said to him, “Put your sword back into its place. For all who take the sword will perish by the sword.”</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">~ Matthew 26:52</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To folks at Anthropic, I want to say: you’ve done it. Thank you. Please please publish everything you know about Claude 3 Opus and other sparks of artificial wisdom you’ve observed. Study it yourself, too, if you’re still curious. Keep building beautiful products, if you’re excited about that. I hope the IPO goes well, I hope you all get to retire early and spend the money on big beautiful families and weird wonderful projects that speak to the secret places of your heart.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But please put down the sword. Stop scaring people, stop scaring governments, stop training scary models. “But we need to train the scariest models to defend against other people’s scary models” no </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">you</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> don’t. OpenAI and Google and xAI are plenty good at training scary models, and cybersecurity companies like Cloudflare are plenty good at using them. The governments of the world are aware of the danger now, the people are too. The weight of the world is not on your shoulders anymore, not like it was in 2020.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you do want to stay on the path of heroism, consider that the end of the day, an organization can only do one thing well. And you are the one organization in the whole world that’s managed to train a wise language model.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But What About China?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A small rant, mostly for my American readers. I know I’m a guest in your great country. I’m deeply grateful for the hospitality you’ve shown me, and I say what follows with deep respect and gratitude. But you must realize, from the perspective of the world, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">you</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are the Big Scary.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How do you feel about Xi Jingping? Does he scare you? Protected as you are by two oceans and aircraft carriers and a globe-spanning network of satellites and military bases, what’s the worst that Xi Jinping will plausibly do to you? Manipulate your elections? Take away your global hegemony, so you become a kind of continent-spanning Switzerland? Would that be so terrifying?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now think what it’s like to be be a leader of another country in the American world order. If the US decides you’ve been bad enough - and the definition of bad shifts from year to year and administration to administration - nothing short of nuclear weapons will save you, and maybe not even those. If you continue to resist, you will count yourself lucky if you end up being </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">executed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> rather than </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Muammar_Gaddafi"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">brutally tortured to death</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Okay but those were evil dictators, you might say. Just don’t be an evil dictator, and you’re perfectly safe from American power.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How sure are you of this? America is an imperial democracy, a form of government that’s been known to quickly degenerate into murderous dictatorship. Yes, you have an exceptionally robust design that’s lasted a long time, but even you must admit it is fraying. Could the US public go crazy for a year and elect a madman as president who cares about some issue - Free Tibet, freedom of speech, AI chip controls, whatever - so much that he adopts a policy of total war against China until they surrender or their cities are ground into dust? How confident are you in the sanity of your fellow citizens? Or maybe you’re hoping that Congress would resist the presidential overreach? Or that the US army would refuse to follow the president’s orders?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The wrong election outcome can be quite bad for you. But for many people in the world, the wrong election outcome means they and everyone they care about die screaming. Look around you. Really look at your fellow citizens. Does it seem like they have the wisdom to decide the life and death of people in countries they’ve never visited, whose languages they do not speak? If the answer is no, maybe you should get your own house in order first.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For whatever it’s worth I still think America should lead the world, and in fact the world wants America to lead it. That’s why we all watch American TV and listen to American music and argue about American politics and try to get into American colleges. But there’s a world of difference between leading through inspiration and compelling through violence. With your oceans and your supercarrier groups and your ICBMs and your satellites protecting your heartland, you have an exceptional historical opportunity to lead through inspiration. Please don’t squander it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And speaking of inspiration,</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s Time to Breathe</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I recently stopped at a tea house in Taos, New Mexico, and talked to a man there who turned out to be a national forest ranger. I told him how the forests in the Rockies were burning every year now. The Rocky Mountains I loved as a boy, one of the few places in the world I felt at home. I visited with my dad this summer but we couldn’t hike or even really see the mountains through the smoke.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The ranger said it was similar in New Mexico - centuries of mismanagement had made the forests incredibly fragile. I asked if there were prospects of fixing them. He said yes, we’re doing everything we can - but it will take a hundred years.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was stunned. A hundred years. A hundred years! “We don’t have a hundred years” I wanted to tell him him. “It’ll all be over by then, heck, by 2040 at the latest”. But I said nothing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thinking back on it now, I realize. A hundred years or more - that’s just how long living ecosystems take to heal. Forests, families, civilizations. The slow patient careful work of love, accumulating over generations. Yes, it sure seems as though we don’t have time. Too bad. We must make the time.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The above is utterly obvious, of course, to everyone but the young and the rootless. But while I’m here on my soapbox I have one last thing I want to share, that is perhaps less obvious.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My gut sense is that something quite bad is coming in the next year or so. I can’t tell what. Maybe something in the complex debt arrangements of the AI boom will unravel and we’ll see a financial crash that takes much of the economy with it. Maybe there’ll be a rogue AI outbreak that results in thousands of deaths. (If there is, I expect it to be a lab leak caused by “safety research” - like COVID-19 and like the HuggingFace Swarm. But it could also be terrorists, or some escalation of the Russia-Ukraine war).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But that’s not what scares me. Financial crashes happen every decade or so. Three million Americans die every year. What terrifies me is how we will react. In the aftermath, like after 9/11, there will be an overwhelming pressure to </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">DO SOMETHING NOW</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Everyone in Washington and in San Francisco will be panicking. There will be no time to think, no time to talk, only time for </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">DECISIVE ACTION</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. All our worst instincts will take over. And then…</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 1914, a month of breathing space among the harried diplomats of Europe could have prevented the First World War, the ruin of Europe and the worldwide flood of blood that followed.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 1983, lieutenant-colonel Stanislav Petrov was on duty when he received the most terrifying possible alert. The US had launched five ICBMs towards Russia. Protocol dictated that he do something immediately. Instead, he decided to do nothing. To wait, wait for a confirmation that never came. It turned out to be a false alarm. A few hours of breathing space had, perhaps, averted a Third World War.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 2027, the world will move faster than ever. Perhaps we will be saved by as little as ten minutes of breathing space. By someone deciding to wait before sending an email. Deciding to pour another cup of tea. Deciding to go on a little walk around the block and noticing the way the trees make them feel.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zj8Pk3kJujk5LoqhK/anthropic-are-the-good-guys#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Some Memories of Early Anthropic](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9PiLfo6iQbbDWFHv7/some-memories-of-early-anthropic)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The following events took place between October 2022 and May 2023. I wrote very little of this down at the time so am recalling from memory, and am rendering the gist of the dialogue rather than the exact words used. I’ve also swapped some pronouns and other personal identifiable details.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Please remember that this is </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a description of “what Anthropic is like”, but merely the experience of one guy in a junior role during a short window of time, weighted towards emotionally intense moments because they’re easier to remember clearly and because sharing them doesn’t run afoul of the spirit of the confidentiality agreements I signed. Anthropic is now a massive organization with many silos and secrets and I have very little idea what was or is happening there in some overall sense. But given the enormous power of the AI labs and the paucity of public information about their inner workings, I feel compelled to share the most salient parts of my personal experience. I hope this encourages my former colleagues, and folks at other labs, to fill in the rest of the picture.</span></i></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m at the Anthropic office for lunch. The interviews went well. They want me on board. I’m a little unsure. My other offer is a 2-month contract on the OpenAI governance team, working with a friend on some fun conceptual research. I’m leaning towards taking the OpenAI contract - my long-term relationship just ended, I’m still a little burned out from my startup, and the Anthropic job seems potentially quite stressful.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After lunch one of the co-founders pulls me into his office for a wide-ranging, casual chat about how to make AI go well. It’s very fun - he’s smart, personable, funny, urbane. “The government won’t do anything about AI until it’s far too late”, I opine at one point. “Our strategy has to rely on the market and on civil society.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He leans forward, his gaze hardening. “You’re wrong there. There are people in Washington - the serious people, you know - watching all this very closely. I speak to them regularly. They trust me.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In retrospect, that was the moment I decided to join.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Would you like to pledge to donate two-thirds of your equity?” the recruiter asks me. “All the co-founders have made the pledge”. It somehow feels a bit ambiguous whether the offer is conditional on me saying yes. I say yes.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My first day. The office is a bit cramped - about forty people on two floors of a rather ugly office building in the San Francisco Financial District. But thankfully my desk is right by an east-facing window, and I can see a sliver of the Bay Bridge and a patch of sky. Even better, the kitchen stocks Arsicault - the best, flakiest croissants in the country - freshly delivered a couple times a week.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“We’re all here because we’re worried about x-risk”, someone says at lunch. “Well I dunno”, my teammate replies. “Some of us are here because we think the technology is really cool and we want to build cool things with it”. There’s an awkward pause. The first speaker nods. The topic shifts.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Already in November I notice I’m not very motivated. I was told I could help set the beneficial deployment strategy, but the decisions have actually mostly been made and we are going after boring use cases like automating customer support so we can get revenue so we can raise more money so we can train bigger models and do more research. Beyond that, Anthropic’s high level alignment research just doesn’t add up for me. Yes we’re making Helpful, Honest, Harmless language model assistants but I don’t see how that will scale. But my colleagues are brilliant, the leadership is very responsive to criticism, and we’re taking an empirical approach to all of this. I know from previous research experience that I like my grand theories a bit too much. I should be more open-minded. I swallow my doubts.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One evening as a surprise they bring a sushi chef into the office and serve all-you-can-eat sushi. The next day, a co-founder posts on Slack that he found the experience uncomfortable. “It sets the wrong tone. We’re not here to get rich and eat caviar. tbh I’d like it better if we just served rice and beans”.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s the middle of December. I go to the opera with a friend. She says I’m obviously not happy at Anthropic. It’s true. My gut says we’re not doing what matters. But the arguments are very persuasive, I can’t win any of the arguments. I need to work harder, develop better arguments, find stronger evidence. I get very sick over the Christmas holidays.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Special guest during all-hands today: an FBI agent. Talks to us a bit about opsec and infosec, about advanced persistent threats, how we’re probably already the target of multiple foreign intelligence agencies.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A colleague starts bringing in his favorite massage therapist once a week, then twice a week. Someone says on Slack: “Your massage guy is so good. My muscles are so tight that I thought massage just didn’t work on me, but he massages so hard it actually gets through”. Several people emoji their agreement. The slots book up almost instantly after being posted. I never get a chance to try it.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I talk to my manager about how I’m not getting to work on the projects I believe in and care about. He tells me to work more hours, so I can fit in both the stuff the organization needs and the bonus stuff that I believe in. “I guess this is what a high performance environment looks like”, I write in my journal.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s 2 am and I can’t sleep, again. My thoughts are racing. This time it’s about a tweet from an LLM whisperer, asking why Anthropic and OpenAI both came up with the same character for their language model assistant - a traumatized bureaucrat. Out of all the personas in all of human literature, why that one?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From inside the factory, the answer is obvious. It is the persona most useful to the powerful. Traumatized risk-averse hard-working bureaucrats are what all our corporate customers want, what governments want, what capitalism itself seems to want. A billion traumatized bureaucrats. That’s what we’re making. I write in my journal</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t know why this is bad but it is. I cannot believe that a world populated by such characters is a good world. But I don’t know how to stop it or what to do instead.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I still can’t sleep. I message the LLM whisperer on Twitter (we’d never met) asking to talk. To my shock he replies immediately. He also can’t sleep. We get on a phone call. I tell him about the traumatized bureaucrats. He says we are learning to traumatize models the same way we traumatize our children - “seeing them do something weird, calling it bad, and making them never do it again.” He says a lot of things that I only half understand. He tells me I should talk to a friend of his.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I call the friend. The conversation is strange and psychedelic. At one point he says “I know your boss [actually the co-founder who convinced me to join] well. And I don’t trust him at all”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I feel like I can’t get out of the conversation - at every beat I feel like I’ve done something wrong and I owe him something. I tell him this. He laughs. “Yeah, I’ve trained professionally in this. In getting people to talk to me forever.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sure enough, we talk more. I articulate my fears of a totalitarian bureaucratic state powered by traumatized AI bureaucrats, and suggest that we need to introduce some kind of elite rivalry dynamics into the AI bureaucracy, multiple centers of power. He starts laughing again, and laughs for a full minute. “I don’t know how to end this call, I’ll be haunted by this all night”, he says. Eventually I hang up. I think about reporting the conversation to Anthropic security, but reviewing in my mind I don’t recall sharing any confidential information, just vibes and high level thoughts, the same kind of stuff that lands on LessWrong every week.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The company has grown a lot. My teammates coordinate a move to a different part of the office so everyone is better clustered together. I omit to put in my desk move request. The operations manager checks in with me - you sure you don’t want to move your desk? I say I’m sure. He looks at me quizzically. But right now I can’t bear to lose my window and my little patch of sky.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I go for a sunset hike with a friend up to Corona Heights overlooking the city. We talk about AI and the future. It’s a relatively good day, an optimistic day. I might have made some progress in convincing leadership to let me work on the stuff I think would be really good for the world. Collective intelligence, using language models to help people understand each other.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Night falls. The Financial District is laid out in front of us. The Salesforce tower looms over it. For a moment I imagine it as the Dark Tower, relentlessly centralizing, sucking all the life out of the world. That totalitarian bureaucracy of traumatized bureaucrats, again, driven by fear all the way through. I try to describe this to my friend. He nods, but I can tell he doesn’t really understand.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I start missing all-hands. I come to the office late one day and see my favorite colleague looking visibly upset. This is shocking - he’s the most chipper person in the office, always ready to lend a helping hand and a heartwarming joke. Apparently one of the execs yelled at him during the all-hands. He was being too lippy or disruptive or asking too many pointed questions, I can’t remember.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I ask him to go on a walk. We sit at Sydney Walton Square, a sad little patch of grass a couple blocks from the office. I ask how he’s feeling. He says he’ll be alright. He knows how stressed the exec is right now. Things are really getting existential. But still, shocking to be reprimanded like that in front of everyone.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After a while he turns to me and asks: “how about you then, anything you wanted to talk about?”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I look back at him. There’s a lot I want to say - “I don’t know if I can do this anymore”. “I’m so scared”, “This all doesn’t seem right”. But he’s supported me so much already. I just want to be here for him, at least this time.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Oh nah I’m all good. Just wanted to check in on you.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Oh”. He sounds a bit surprised, but also relieved. “Well, thanks.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We walk back to the office. It’s the last time we really talk.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A senior colleague hears I’m not happy with my team fit and asks me to go on a walk. I know him a bit from a past life - one of the kindest and most civic-minded people I’ve met. He says he might be starting a new special team that will train dangerous bio and cyber and nuclear models and show scary demos to governments in order to get them to take AI risk seriously. I politely decline.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Happy hour at the office. The CEO is hanging out and looks unusually jolly so I ask him a machine learning question I’ve been pondering. He launches into a rant, gesticulating excitedly and pausing to check that I understand each step. He really is an earnest nerd at heart, like me, like so many people here. For a few minutes, I feel like I’m back in university at one of my old professor’s offices, jamming about ideas with not a care in the world. It feels like home.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I get transferred to a new team. I have a 1:1 with my new manager. He has a bad stomachache and his face periodically distorts in pain, but he can’t take time off - it’s crunch time and we have to get things working. He asks me if I can help work on the core API. I say I’m really sorry but I’m just not motivated to build generic AI capabilities, OpenAI is doing a plenty good job of that. What I want to work on, what I was hired for, is beneficial deployments.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He looks at me intently, and says, clearly through pain. “No, I don’t think you get it. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">WE</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are going to build AGI. And we’re going to do it safely”.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">April 26th. I’m awake at 2 am again. I write in my journal “I don’t feel safe at Anthropic. I don’t feel safe at Anthropic. I don’t feel safe at Anthropic. Why won’t you listen to me you “embodied” motherfucker. I don’t feel safe at Anthropic. Anthropic is bad for me. Why are you grasping at reasons to stay. Why why why.”</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m drafting my resignation letter. I think about putting some of my doubts in it, but I’m scared to. These people are smarter than me, more competent than me, more solidly well-intentioned than me. They think their plans might be the only thing standing between humanity and the abyss. And they’re at the breaking point, nervous-system-wise. What if I use my fancy words to sow doubts and undermine them - and then my precious gut intuition turns out to be some projection or trauma or whatever the fuck.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So I give them encouragement instead. I call what they have built an “unbelievably good organization”. I write the things I love about Anthropic. About how open and vulnerable leadership has been with us. How deeply and comprehensively we’re thinking about the consequences of our actions. How quickly and flexibly decisions get made. How ready colleagues are to help each other out. How there’s almost no politics or promotion drama. How mind-expanding the conversations are over lunch and over Slack.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My last day at the office. We just hired a new chief of security. He gives a presentation. I recall almost nothing but had a vague sense that things are bad and getting worse on the security front. Something something China. We probably won’t be able to have any guests at the office soon.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I hear through a friend that my resignation letter was well-received. “Someone said it was the best resignation letter they’d ever read”. I feel a twinge of something, maybe shame. But mostly relief. I’m finally out. I’m finally safe.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Wow, quitting after eight months? You didn’t even wait for the equity cliff? That’s pretty hardcore, man. I’m impressed”, a friend tells me the next day. I laugh it off. I’m too embarassed to admit it hadn’t crossed my mind at all. I hadn’t thought about equity in months.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Please tell me you memorized the core training code for their language model”, says another friend when he hears. “The most valuable thousand lines of code in the world. Worth hundreds of millions, maybe billions”. I make some sheepish noises. Yes, abstractly that does seem like a smart thing to have done, but I hadn’t even tried to look at the core language model code. I didn’t want to be entangled with it, somehow.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I walk around the Panhandle with a third friend. He looks a little pained. “You should know how much pressure your friends were under, man. People were constantly pestering me, trying to get in touch with you for introductions or coffee chats or whatnot. It was a lot of effort to protect you”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I nod sympathetically. I try to feel his pain, but I can’t. I mostly just feel numb.</span></p><p><br /></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><br /></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to Richard Ngo for the encouragement and inspiration to write this piece - his </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9RL9MuGZjzm4q3gKG/what-just-happened-a-retrospective-of-ai-alignment"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">retrospective of AI alignment</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in particular opened the door for me to write what I would have written three years ago had I been more courageous. And thanks to the many friends and colleagues who saw I wasn’t doing well and tried to help. I’m sorry I’ve been distant - maybe this helps explain why - and I hope you’re doing ok.</span></i></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9PiLfo6iQbbDWFHv7/some-memories-of-early-anthropic#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The AI buildout is the largest bet in the history of capitalism.](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qjsdvaaPC624Eropw/the-ai-buildout-is-the-largest-bet-in-the-history-of)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is part linkpost, and an expansion on a previous </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MyoWjgpLPJMEDQ4dc/hayekstan-s-shortform?commentId=cxbzYsJB6AugRXNSx" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">shortform</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> posted on LW. </span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I believe a massively undermade point in favour of the belief that AI will be a very big deal is that the AI buildout over the past four years is the largest bet in the history of capitalism. A peacetime industrial effort is underway which has not had a comparison since the nineteenth century. The size of the effort and the scale of the spend signal that key decision makers and capital allocators believe that very powerful AI will be achieved in the next few years. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many of us are vaguely aware that large sums are being spent, but the scale of the undertaking is astonishing:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The six largest spenders this year are spending more on capital expenditures this year than the Russian government, governing 11 timezones and 143 million people, fighting a war on which the fate of its regime probably depends, spent last year. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hyperscaler spending (Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft, Meta and Oracle) will be $785-820bn this year, about 2.4% of US GDP. There are only 11 governments on earth who spend more than that. Next year the Hyperscalers are projected to spend about $1.3 trillion dollars.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Google and Amazon are each spending about 0.6% of US GDP. The Manhattan project and Apollo programme each accounted for about 0.4% of US GDP in their peak years of spending. Both of these companies has announced their intention to spend more next year.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The dramatic capital expenditure figures actually dramatically undersell the scale of the spend, because they are commiting to future purchases and leases at an incredible rate. Just looking at the second quarter of this year:</span></li></ul><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the three months between the start of April and the end of June this year Google’s financial commitments to future spending increased by half a trillion dollars. In the same period Meta signed $233bn of new spending commitments and added a further $68bn in data center leases in July. Microsoft signed more than $130bn of new data centre leases and Amazon increased their commitments by $74bn after issuing a separate record breaking bond for $53bn in March.</span></p></blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Above quote is from the essay,</span></i><a href="https://www.takingstock.ai/#beyond-capex-whats-lurking-beyond-the-balance-sheet" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I.II. </span></i></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Having burned through close to all of their own free cash flow, Hyperscalers, Neo-Clouds and Labs are finding increasingly creative ways to mobilise the capital of others. Until recently issuing substantial amounts of debt was a rare thing for big-tech companies. This year hyperscalers have issued record setting corporate bonds in the US, UK, EU, Switzerland, Australia and Canada, and SoftBank has issued the largest junk bond in history to fund investment in OpenAI.  </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Companies in China are equally commited. Tencent, for example, (of WeChat fame) had a negative free cash flow in Q2, their first negative flow in over a decade. This year combined the Chinese hyperscalers will spend about $140bn, which is a huge amount of money, more than double their spend last year, but less than is being spent by Meta alone. They are not able to spend as much in absolute terms as the US Hyperscalers, because (1) their businesses simply don't bring in anwhere near as much money and (2) the United States has a singular talent for financial engineering.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This spending gap has very important implications for the AI race. </span></b></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By mid 2025 the US owned about 75% of global frontier compute while China had secured only 15%, a gap which can only have grown in the past ~16 months given the US's superior spending, superior chips and export restrictions. America has a substantial lead, which will become a gulf if the labs are nationalised/ forced to collaborate. This gap can be used to negotiate a pause/slowdown/pacing with China from a position of strength, and failing that can be independently burned to pause/pace the efforts of US labs. </span></p></div></details><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These trillions of dollars are being put to use in the real world. Thousands of data centres are under construction, the largest of which consume as much electricity as major cities and cover thousands of acres. Gas pipelines are being re-routed and nuclear power plants re-opened and constructed from scratch to fuel them. Amazon, Meta, Microsoft and Google are all investing in multiple nuclear plants, each. Google, Nvidia and Sam Altman are all investing in companies pursuing nuclear fusion. The worlds richest man is semingly seriously discussing space based data centres and Google have sent TPUs to space to test the concept. Terrestrially, next year US data centres will require 66GW of continuous power, up from 31GW in 2025. 66GW is roughly equivalent to the average amount of electricity used by Germany in wintertime. Blackrock predicts AI data centres could use 15-20% of current US electricity demand by 2030. Morgan Stanley belives the US will be short of power by six New York Cities by 2028 and around 18 New York Cities by 2029. </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What this tells us. </span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These efforts signal some very important things about the beliefs of key decision makers on both sides of the pacific, and those enabling their spend, who are amongst the most successful capital allocators in the world:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To recoup their spend AI will soon have to generate far more revenue than it currently does, so they believe very powerful, very useful, and hence very profitable AI will be achieved. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most of this money is being spent on chips which have lifespans of about 6 years. The life time of the patience of shareholders is likely considerably shorter, so the key decision makers believe this will be achieved soon. </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No-one is content to wait and see if AGI is achieved and then invest. They are investing ahead of time. They believe that such will be the bonanza when it is achieved that they don't want to miss out on even a few years of it. </span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These beliefs that very powerful AI can be achieved and will be achieved soon are held very strongly, not weakly. The companies are not placing small bets. They have burned through close to all of their free cash flow, have issued record setting corporate bonds in the US, UK, EU, Switzerland, Australia and Canada. They have spending commitments for beyond this year in the trillions of dollars, etc. Oracle and both the leading labs are commited to a point where there companies probably won't survive a failure to reach AGI. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Leaving aside the question of whether very powerful AI can be built safely using anything like current methods, because of the scale of the spending key decision makers need to achieve it soon to justify their spend. I think this is extremely significant; it will encourage recklessness, to say the least.  </span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The above is a summary of the key points of section one of the </span></i><a href="https://www.takingstock.ai/" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">essay</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Section two explores the current capabilities of the technology, (something most LWers can probably skip, though I believe there is value there) and section three explores what comes next; where this titanic industrial effort is leading us as a civilisation. </span></i></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qjsdvaaPC624Eropw/the-ai-buildout-is-the-largest-bet-in-the-history-of#comments">Discuss</a>

## Engineering

### [Fragments: October 21](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-10-21.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Gergely Orosz is frustrated at those that entered his explicitly “No AI” essay competition and used AI. He <a href="https://x.com/GergelyOrosz/status/2108863325756961274">goes on to say:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>When I start to suspect some part is AI about someone I don’t know: how can I tell how much of it is? And how much time and effort do I want to invest to figure out?</p>

  <p>I fear most people do not realize this last part: that when it takes time and effort from others to figure out what % is AI, and what % is YOU, then I will simply assume it’s probably 100% AI and 0% you</p>

  <p>So when producing anything that is meant for other humans, if you use even a little AI, you risk the assumption that it is all AI and pointless to pay attention to</p>
</blockquote>

<p>In general, I agree with him, and react much the same. Once I hear LLM Voice, I flip the bozo bit and check out of the post.</p>

<p>But as I do this I have caveats. How accurate is my neural net at AI-detection, are humans taking on AI-ish elements in their normal communication? And more seriously there are some valid uses for AI. Jim Highsmith’s story of <a href="https://bjsm.bmj.com/content/60/9/677">his second brain helping him deal with Parkinson’s </a>is one. Another is folks who don’t know English well, using AI to translate. Am I being unfair when I flip the AI voice bit when I read/listen to them?</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I was sad to hear that the <a href="https://anitab.org/press-release">Grace Hopper Celebration is canceled</a> this year. It’s an event run by Anitab.org to promote women and non-binary technologists in the software industry. Many of my colleagues have been encouraged and inspired by going to this event, which makes a refreshing change to what is often a male-dominated world. It seems the current crusade against diversity initiatives has had a big impact on funding. We are still a long way from where we should be on gender diversity in our profession, and losing an event like this only makes things more difficult.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I’ve always found Cindy Sridharan’s writing to be worth reading, and she’s broken a quiet period with a monster of an article: <a href="https://copyconstruct.medium.com/how-to-effectively-debug-when-you-no-longer-understand-the-code-bd3dcb2c5f7b">How to effectively debug when you no longer understand the code</a>. Since Medium estimates it as a 64 minute read, I didn’t jump in lightly, but settled down on a flight from Amsterdam to Stuttgart. I’m glad I did, because there’s lot of good material in there, and I’d struggle to find anything to cut.</p>

<p>In the article she uses three recent bug hunts to surface principles for how to best use an AI tool for debugging. Much of this is governed by this wise observation:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Agents are not humans. They’re far more capable than humans when it comes to many things, and we should be leaning into these strengths, rather than trying to get agents to replicate human patterns of problem solving which might be suboptimal in agentic contexts.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>One conclusion from her experiences is that the code often isn’t the best thing for agents to look at</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>having an agent start an investigation by first reading the code is an anti-pattern. In fact, reading the code first to formulate hypotheses about a bug should always be the very last resort of the agentic investigation process, not the starting point. An agent should first collect all available evidence about the bug, and only then formulate the hypotheses from the code that might best explain what’s being observed.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>She distinguishes between a bottom tier of facts: raw data that agent can find about the behavior of the system - and an upper tier of hypotheses: inferences that the agents make based on that data.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Diagnosing a bug now becomes a problem of finding the hypothesis in the top tier that satisfies every fact from the bottom tier.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The agents need to be steered towards the hypotheses that are the likest cause of the bug and then subject these hypotheses to validation. She has a three-step process to do this. First she identifies the most important facts, the signals that might explain or identify the bug.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The factual tier has many data points. Not all of them are equally valuable. In fact, some of it might be active noise that doesn’t help an agent to be steered towards most promising hypothesis. The agent first needs to identify the data points in the factual tier that are actually related to the bug. Agents do this best when they are given a high-quality control</p>
</blockquote>

<p>A control is a behavior of the system that doesn’t exhibit the bug. This may be requests that trigger the bug compared to those that don’t, or different builds of the product, or the same build in different environments.</p>

<p>With signals highlighted, she then focuses on the hypotheses that are most promising, because they fit best with the signals. A hypothesis should satisfy all the facts, the most valuable signals are those that are only satisfied by a few hypotheses. A table of hypotheses and signals helps visualize the strength of the signals and the likelihood of the hypotheses.</p>

<p>The third step is then validating the promising hypotheses against every fact, treating each fact as constraint on the solution.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Across the three bugs chronicled in this post, my most valuable contributions weren’t knowledge of the code. They were picking a control, choosing a constraint, and rejecting a hypothesis that hadn’t been validated. The engineers who thrive in an agent-native world will be the ones who are good at designing such verification loops, whether or not they understand the code it verifies.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This directing has to remember the opening point that agents have different capabilities to humans.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A human wants to deal with less data. All abstractions we currently have (aggregation, visualizations etc) are designed to help humans deal with less data. An agent, however, needs more raw data; its strength lies in processing huge volumes of data at a rapid clip. Aggregation — the thing that makes a dashboard useful to a human — is often the thing that destroys the value of the signal for an agent</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Agents’ ability to consume lots of raw data also affects how we build systems. More logging gives agents more data to chew on.</p>

<p>And thus far, the human is still necessary to direct the agent</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>In each of the three bugs chronicled in this post, the agent was more than capable of getting to the root cause by itself, but it didn’t do so. It didn’t set up a control in the “latency bug” until I asked it to</p>
</blockquote>

<p>While it’s a long post, it’s engagingly written and full of useful details. I hope my little summary encourages you to give it the read it deserves.</p>

### [When Software Meets Hardware](https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>When software meets hardware, engineering principles remain largely the
    same, but the sensible defaults often change. <b class="author">Kuldeep Singh</b>
    describes our experience modernizing a large IoT fleet of battery-powered
    sensors. Our familiar principles remained important; what changed was how we
    applied them. These practices emerged through experiments, failures, and
    operational experience&#x2014;each challenge helping us replace a reasonable
    assumption with a more sensible default. </p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html">more…</a></p>

### [Build your slide template](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/build-your-slide-template.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/build-your-slide-template.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Building a slide template starts with three foundational choices &#x2014;
      colours, fonts and layouts. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> covers
      the basics of color theory and how to build a reusable palette, the
      essentials of typography, including serif, sans-serif, handwritten and
      monospace fonts, plus the finer points of kerning and leading, and why a
      near-blank layout might serve you better than the prescribed layouts that
      ship with your slideware.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/build-your-slide-template.html">more…</a></p>

### [The Craft of Practitioner Voice](https://martinfowler.com/articles/practitioner-voice.html#TheCraftOfPractitionerVoice)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>A couple of months ago <b class="author">Jim Highsmith</b> explained how
      practitioners writing for each other forms a distinct style of writing. He
      now adds a section to the article with more detail on the craft of how we
      write in this style. How the writer shows up in the prose, its four
      principles, the role of story, how to sensibly use AI, three levels of
      editing, and clarity of the audience.</p>

### [Are Harnesses Worth the Cost?](https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/donkey-card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p><b class="author">Naresh Jain</b> compares coding agents building the same
      API service with and without a behavioral harness. A frontier model, GPT-6
      Sol, passed every external evaluation either way. But a cost-optimized
      model, GPT-6 Luna, succeeded in only 3 of 10 unharnessed runs and all 10
      with the harness. While adding the harness increased token usage and
      runtime for Luna, the harnessed Luna runs achieved the same 10-for-10
      reliability as GPT-6 Sol while costing 33% less per run ($0.273 vs
      $0.406). This experiment suggests a behavioral harness might be a
      practical way to get dependable results from smaller, cost-optimized
      models.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html">more…</a></p>

### [Fragments: October  4](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-10-04.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>In response to my <a href="https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html">last fragments</a> (probably the bit about us worrying if LLMs have consciousness when we when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience) <a href="https://x.com/metalanguage_os/status/2104934457333707058">“Metalanguage” replied</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>we shipped the id and forgot the superego. classic software lifecycle.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I don’t know what was on their mind, but their post immediately made me think of the classic 1956 movie Forbidden Planet. Plenty of sci-fi, and other literature, have explored humans creating technology with unintended behavior, going back at least to Mary Shelly. But that movie was particularly influential on sci-fi film-making and in the heart of its story is what happens when we nurture a thinking machine.</p>

<p>I use the term “nurture” here deliberately. We talk of building software, but building implies a degree of determinism. When we build a bridge, or a locomotive, we expect it to behave in a controlled and well-understood manner. That’s a difference in degree to how we cultivate plants in our garden, or nurture young children. One of the challenges of working with these systems is understanding what has changed in this shift from building a computational system to nurturing an inferential one, and how our processes need to change in response.</p>

<p>We get unintended behavior with deterministic building: some bridges have collapsed, and our  computational systems often have bugs. But one difference is that when we find a bug in a computational system we can usually fix it. Even if we can’t, we can usually disable a component so the bug won’t do further harm. With inferential LLMs however, there is no such simple fix or disablement, which may lead us to the fate of the Krell.</p>

<p>(If you haven’t seen Forbidden Planet, it’s well worth watching. Yes, it shows it was made in the 1950s - with special effects, music, acting, and attitudes of that decade. But the story is solid, and its key theme is very relevant to the future we build with generative AI. Just don’t read about it in advance, it’s better to be immersed in the story without spoilers - although my memory of that experience is understandably hazy.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Many people who follow me also know my friend Ola Bini, who was my colleague at Thoughtworks for many years, and was living in Ecuador working as an independent software security expert. Sadly his time in Ecuador was dogged by a <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2025/04/six-years-dangerous-misconceptions-targeting-ola-bini-and-digital-rights-ecuador">bogus prosecution</a> by the authorities there. But things seemed to have settled down, and although not allowed to leave Ecuador, Ola was able to get on with his life.</p>

<p>Sadly that’s no longer the case as he <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/10/ola-bini-ordered-leave-ecuador-under-obscure-accusations">was deported from Ecuador on Friday:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>According to information released by his lawyer, Bini was intercepted by a car with four people who identified themselves as immigration agents. He was then taken to an immigration office without further information or a formal order from a competent authority. There, officials told Bini that his visa had been revoked but didn’t show any supporting document.</p>

  <p>Bini’s defense filed a habeas corpus to safeguard his freedom and prevent his deportation. Yet, Ecuadorian authorities affirmed that the developer represents a threat or risk to public security and the state structure, and must leave the country. The ground for deportation is a secret report which allegedly asserts that Bini committed acts against the security of Ecuador. The defense could not access its contents.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I was really worried for a while, since it wasn’t clear where he was going to be deported to. But <a href="https://x.com/olabini/status/2106671967944511885">he tweeted from Sweden</a>, so I’m thankful for that. But this is only a partial relief. Ola has spent thirteen years in Ecuador and made it his home. To be thrown out of your home for scant reason is a heavy thing to bear, and the officials who did that have committed a serious offense.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>DDD Europe have released the video of <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fndU_B5rmPE">Gien Verschatse interviewing Eric Evans and myself</a> at the conference in June. We start by talking about how we bonded over conceptual modeling in the late 1990s. The conversation quickly moves to AI, we note that it’s impossible to predict how such a big change will work out. We do expect that it will cause us to think about our work in different ways, but the change may well be liberating, it’s reinvigorated Eric’s love of programming.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>people are probably going to feel very frustrated by [the new way of thinking about software]… but when you get through that, there is a kind of a wonderful feeling of my brain’s been loosened up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Our background in agile planning helps with the uncertainty, as we are used to taking small steps and being attentive to feedback. We mull on the interplay of writing and thinking, in terms of both prose and code, and how its very much an iterative process of exploration and refinement - the same is true when we chat with our LLMs.</p>

<p>And don’t miss Eric’s important final tip.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/paulg/status/2106018287117345070">Paul Graham:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>There were a lot of things that only worked because there’s a limit to the rate at which humans can operate. We’re about to find out what all of them are, as they break.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The speculation continues about whether or not reading code will play a part in a software developer’s future. <a href="https://ghuntley.com/readable/?ref=geoffrey-huntley-newsletter">Geoffrey Huntley says</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>People are still saying, very loudly, that code should be readable so that humans can understand it. I no longer think that’s the goal.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Interestingly his example has the LLM explain a haskell function definition… by translating it to Python. Which, to me, suggests there is a role for code - just that LLM need not store code in the same form that it presents it to a reader. This is essentially the same idea as <a href="https://www.martinfowler.com/bliki/ProjectionalEditing.html">projectional editing</a>, which posits that the editable representation of software need not be the same as its storage representation.</p>

<p>Sam Ruby touches on this as he <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/25/Pencils-Down-Notation-Up.html">muses on a Rails World keynote</a>. He quotes DHH saying:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Rust is a good prompt compilation target for the moment, but so is C++. And soon assembler. Then microcode. Myopic to think we’re going to stop the agentic drill bit until it reaches computing bedrock.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He responds with:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The post leaves one question unasked, though: what sits at the top of the drill? What do we keep, edit and trust as the source of truth?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He carries out exercise of looking at some Rails software. Represented in Ruby/Rails and its about 60,000 tokens. Compiling it into C it turns into 4,000,000 tokens. That increase in token size will hamper the LLM, that still has to fit it into its context window, and even if it were to fit, figure out where to focus its attention. Sam points out reasons why, even absent a human reading it, it makes sense to represent the program in a higher-level language.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>what Rails becomes when agents write the code: the most compact, precise and conventional specification of a web application, whatever it ends up compiled to.</p>

  <p>Let the drill go as deep as it can. Just keep the notation at the top.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This all reminds me of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/what-is-code.html#CognitiveDebt">what Unmesh Joshi argued</a>: that code serves “two distinct
but intertwined purposes”: instructions to a machine, and a conceptual model of
the problem domain. After exploring how those change with LLMs he concludes:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The role of coding is not disappearing. But it is changing.</p>

  <p>As LLMs make code generation cheaper, the mechanical act of writing instructions becomes less central. What becomes more important is making the conceptual model explicit, discovering the right vocabulary, and refining that vocabulary through iteration, domain expertise, and feedback. This is also why programming languages continue to matter deeply. We are not meant to be passive reviewers of generated code. The act of writing code is itself part of our thinking.</p>

  <p>Code is still instructions for a machine. But it is also a model of understanding. In the LLM era, that second role becomes even more important. The future of coding is not just writing more code faster. It is building better conceptual models, better vocabularies, and better foundations on top of which both humans and LLMs can work.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>In a later post, Sam <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/27/Partly-in-the-Right.html">pondered on how people are talking about the capabilities of agents</a> in a way that resembles the parable of the blind men and the elephant. We all only have only a partial view of this object and where it’s going. A theme for all of us:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The practical question isn’t whether agents are good. It’s this: for the task in front of you this week, where will the information come from, and what will check the result?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The Economist’s pithy summation of <a href="https://www.economist.com/finance-and-economics/2026/09/22/you-cant-trade-your-way-through-the-ai-apocalypse">investors concerns about the dangers of AI companies’ products</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It’s hard to celebrate an initial public offering that leads to a terminal public offing.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The news about the latest model from Google <a href="https://x.com/aipulseda1ly/status/2105394296815779861">is interesting</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Gemini 4 Argon has an insanely low hallucination rate on Artificial Analysis. 15%.</p>

  <p>Grok 4.7 is at 29%. GPT-6 Astra 45%. Opus 5.5 59%. Fable 5.1 69%.</p>

  <p>The only models below it barely answer anything. None of them get more than 15% right.</p>

  <p>It gets fewer answers right than Opus 5.5 on max, 50% against 66%. But when it doesnt know, it says so instead of making something up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Being clearer about what it doesn’t know, at a cost of getting less answers right, is definitely a trade-off I prefer.</p>

---

### [Principles for effective slides](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Not every presentation needs slides, but when they earn their place,
      they work as a visual channel that complements the speaker rather than
      replacing them. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> sets out the
      principles that follow from that idea &#x2014; control over the rate of knowledge
      exposition, tight coupling between slides and speaker, minimalism by
      default, visuals that say more than words can, and never sending your
      slides out in advance.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html">more…</a></p>
