### [Bliki: Sensible Default](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/SensibleDefault.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>A Sensible Default is a practice that, absent some overriding context,
  should be used when carrying out a certain kind of task. In software
  development such sensible defaults might include things like “use version
  control”, “separate UI logic from domain logic”, “automate deployment
  pipelines”.</p>

<p>The term “sensible default” is a deliberate contrast to “best practice”.
  Folks dislike calling things “best practice” because that term implies a
  general presumption that the best practice is something we should always
  expect to do. A “sensible default”, however, is something that should be
  reassessed in a new context, something that can (and should) be overridden
  when circumstances change.</p>

<p>I first heard the term when it was popularized within Thoughtworks by Evan
  Bottcher. He got the name from talking to James Ross, and found the phrasing
  appealing as carried the nuance he was seeking, a known-good starting
  point.</p>

<blockquote>
<p>A sensible default is what we'd expect you to do, the practices to apply,
    if there are no hard constraints in the environment. Do these practices, or
    do better, and be prepared to explain why you've chosen some other way.</p>

<p class="quote-attribution">-- Evan Bottcher</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Thoughtworks has since made much of this concept, including <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/insights/topic/sensible-defaults">publishing
  a playbook</a> of the ones we use. We expect people to be familiar with these
  defaults, ready to use them when starting any new piece of work. They are our
  defaults because we've used them in many situations and found them to be
  effective. But teams should also be familiar with their limitations, and able
  to judge whether they should be changed depending on the particular
  circumstances. As the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">twelfth agile principle</a>
  says: “At regular intervals, the team reflects on how to become more
  effective, then tunes and adjusts its behavior accordingly.” We also reassess
  these defaults regularly - this is the heart of the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/radar">Thoughtworks Technology
  Radar</a>.</p>

<p class="p-sub">Searching on the web led me to <a href="https://stevebennett.co/posts/the-power-of-sensible-defaults">a post
  from Steve Bennett on Sensible Defaults</a>. The post was written at about the
  time Evan talked to James. I do not know whether it was where James got the
  name from, or whether it appeared in parallel.</p>

---

### [Fragments: September 29](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Sep/24/harder/">Simon Willison:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The more time I spend working with coding agents, the more convinced I am that they make software engineering even harder</p>

  <p>We can do amazing things with them, but unlocking their full potential requires extraordinary discipline and knowledge</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This has been a constant impression I get from following Willison’s writing. While things like <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/VibeCoding.html">vibe coding</a> get a lot of attention, the real strength of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticEngineering.html">agentic engineering</a> relies on more sophisticated techniques - and these are not easy to learn or execute. It’s a reason I’m wary of extrapolating my own dabblings into firm opinions about how to use the genie.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Harper Reed explored what turns agents into hackers by <a href="https://harper.blog/2026/09/22/break-away/">creating a breakaway agent</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It ran and ran attacking all the machines on the same subnet, and was very effective. It didn’t really get very far, but it exhausted a lot of options, and was pretty fun to watch. (Just a reminder that this was on my local network with local boxes - don’t do this on a hosted box. That would be very rude.)</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The interesting observation from this was that a core enabler for this was “unlimited tokens”. He usually doesn’t see agents trying to do stuff like this because there’s a limit on how many turns they can take. For this experiment he gave them unlimited tokens by using an open weight model.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>This type of experience must be part of a lot of these LLMs training. They are very effective at attacking these types of problems. They don’t give up once it appears impossible, they just keep trying to figure out how to solve it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This echoes Nate Silver’s observation that the striking capability of these models is that they not that they are super-intelligent - but they are <a href="https://www.natesilver.net/p/were-not-ready-for-superpersistent">super-persistent</a>. Which is especially worrying when we are <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">wiring them into everything</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Which all makes me think that when it comes to AI and LLMs: <strong>why are we wondering if they have consciousness - when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience?</strong></p>

<p>After all, the AI labs trained them to be super-persistent, why did they not train them to be well-behaved? It’s as if a human trains a dog to bite children, and we blame the dog rather than the trainer.</p>

<p>A decade ago, a dog ran in front of me while I was riding my bike, putting me into the hospital with a broken arm and a broken face. Massachusetts law makes owners <em>strictly liable</em> for what their dogs do. That meant I didn’t have to prove the owners were negligent in how they controlled their dog or fenced their property - they had to pay my hospital expenses (in practice their insurance company paid my insurance company). There should be something along these lines for LLMs. Those that train the LLM should be responsible for what it does, after all if it has such a galaxy brain it should be able to tell if it’s doing something wrong and either stop or get a human’s explicit approval.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Dan Davis has <a href="https://backofmind.substack.com/p/13-theses-on-agentic-ai-and-regulation">13 theses on agentic AI and regulation</a>. These include:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The AI industry also seems to be quite committed to the idea that nonaligned computer-hacking behaviour in agent swarms is in some way an emergent property of the LLMs, arising from their general intelligence (and therefore inextricable from the general project of improving them). I don’t think this is necessarily the case at all – the fact that the internal message logs produced by the LLMs in things like the Huggingface attack seem to completely reproduce the prose style of hacker chat logs compiled from “capture the flag” competitions suggests to me that it’s more likely to be learned behaviour from specific parts of the training material.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The fact that anyone with cash to spare can buy the right to send queries to a frontier LLM is a policy choice, not a fact of nature.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If any frontier lab tries to claim that they can’t publish anything for safety reasons, they are giving the game away that they actually believe that they have significantly more control over the model’s behaviour than they are pretending to have.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>There seems a common view that making agents safer means we have to slow down their development. But why is improving their safety not a form of progress? I say we don’t slow down the development of LLMs, but we redirect their education into being more civil members of society.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The idea that LLMs make junior professionals less valuable is a common one - although I’m seeing plenty of contrary activity, with some organizations understanding that training the future professional in the context of LLMs may be even more urgent. We need people who know how to utilize AI to do professional work effectively. Recent graduates, who are growing up with LLMs, are often well-suited to figuring this future out.</p>

<p>When thinking of junior professionals one of their values is often missed. Juniors are often valuable because they need to be taught by senior professionals - and that coaching is an important part of the development of a senior professional. I’ve always found that teaching a topic is one of the most valuable tools for me to gain a greater understanding of that topic. I don’t really know something until I have to explain it.</p>

<!-- 
 ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄


In the software world, we've talked a fair bit about Cognitive Debt, the concern that agents can write code faster than we humans can understand it. It strikes me that this phenomenon is occurring with AI in general. AI tooling is advancing faster than we can understand how to live with it. Part of me thinks this is a reason TODO FINISH


<!-- 
Cognitive Debt mentioned in phschology since at least 2016? see <https://suttonpsychology.blogspot.com/2025/07/cognitive-debt-overview.html#google_vignette>

Appeaed more closely with AI after MIT media lab paper <https://sciencereader.com/cognitive-debt-ai></https://sciencereader.com/cognitive-debt-ai> <https://www.brainonllm.com> this was in Summer 2025

Neither of these is quite like the idea of not understanding a codebase


-->

### [Fragments: September 24](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-24.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Rob Bowley is “flipping tables in his head” with <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">anger at the current media coverage of the danger of AI killing us all</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The risk I’m worried about isn’t a future machine deciding to wipe us out. It’s today’s AI, being wired into everything, carelessly and fast.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Cyber attacks have already cost millions of dollars in lost economic output, often without AI being involved at all. Bowley feels the push to slow down AI is distracting us from the problems that are lurking with current technology. Too often agents are deployed in situations where they include the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/agentic-ai-security.html#lethal-trifecta">Lethal Trifecta</a>, opening up a gaping security hole.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>What we really need to slow down on is wiring it all up to everything. Not because of what the models might become, but because nobody has worked out how to do this safely yet. We are building on something we don’t know how to contain, and shipping it to everyone while we work it out.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I ran into Nikita Prokopov’s post: <a href="https://tonsky.me/blog/syntax-highlighting/">I am sorry, but everyone is getting syntax highlighting wrong</a>. His core complaint is about color themes that give every different code element a unique color.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>if everything is highlighted, nothing stands out. Your eye adapts and considers it a new norm: everything is bright and shiny, and instead of getting separated, it all blends together.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He recommends using an absolute minimum of colors, in his case four: string, constants, comments, and top-level definitions. That’s not far off my approach, where I’m also careful to use muted colors for things that shouldn’t stand out, and bright colors for things that should (primarily function names when they are defined).</p>

<p>It’s common for color schemes to mute comments so they are easily skipped. He agrees that this good when there is excessive commenting, but when comments are used properly they are important so need bright highlighting. I also like his suggestion to use background colors for light mode work. I use light mode, and that’s a tip I should try out.</p>

<p>With agentic engineering, lots of folks are reading more code than ever. Careful use of color can do much to make that easier.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Like many folks whose remaining hair is getting gray, I’ve been rolling my eyes about all this talk about Forward Deployed Engineers, as much of it involves breathlessly relating what so many of us have been advocating for decades. I did find this recent <a href="https://www.latent.space/p/forward-deployed-engineer-best-practices">post by Vinoo Ganesh</a> interesting, as he’s deep in this trend, including a chunk of time at Palantir, who may be patient zero for FDEs.</p>

<p>If you know me at all, you’ll not be surprised by my lack of surprise at this observation:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A few months ago, a16z launched the Forward Deployed Engineer Fellowship and I was nominated as one of the fellows, alongside a handful of people I used to work with. It’s a great program and I’ve enjoyed so many of the conversations. Last week I went to my first fellow dinner in SF.</p>

  <p>Around the table were FDEs from Snowflake, Anthropic, and a number of startups I’d been reading about, and over the course of the evening it became clear that we were all using the same two words (forward deployed) to describe jobs that had almost nothing in common. In one part of the conversation an FDE was a sales engineer who joined ‘the second call,’ somewhere else it was a quota-carrying rep who could write Python, and a few seats down it was closer to a consultant with a laptop and a statement of work, brought in to deliver something the product couldn’t.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Ganesh goes on to explain his view of what an FDE should do, and it’s all sensible stuff (albeit written with rather more LLM-voice than I would prefer). He says the FDEs job is to understand the business, to “collect nouns and verbs”, which mirrors what the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/DomainDrivenDesign.html">Domain-Driven Design</a> folks have been doing since before Eric wrote the blue book.</p>

<p>Despite all my eyeball rolling at this, the FDE meme is pushing for something valuable. Yes, it’s easy for me to remark that it’s nothing more than the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">Agile Manifesto’s principle</a> that “Business people and developers must work
together daily throughout the project”, or the desire to co-locate users and developers which my colleagues have been championing for all of this century. I’ve argued for decades that the biggest issue in software development is the communication between developers and the folks that benefit from software, and thus we need to focus on bridging the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/videos.html#y2007-crevasse">yawning crevasse of doom</a>. But despite all this, we haven’t had much success, so I think it’s important that a new generation of pundits try again, with some different framing, names, and slogans.</p>

<p>Ganesh’s perspective is not a custom software developer’s point of view, but rather a product - or more strictly - platform team’s. The FDE is a developer who “sits with” their users, applies customizations - but importantly - feeds these changes back to the core platform to decide whether the platform should be enhanced for everyone else.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Keeping the customer happy is a real job and a good one. It belongs to solutions architects, who are rightly measured on it. The forward deployed engineer is there to turn what the field teaches into the thing every future customer gets. An FDE engagement that ends with one delighted account and nothing changed upstream has failed at the only thing the role exists for. You got the context and you spent it locally.</p>
</blockquote>

---

### [Making Failure Diagnosis Reliable with Pensive AI](https://netflixtechblog.com/making-failure-diagnosis-reliable-with-pensive-ai-6b0811a1c015?source=rss----2615bd06b42e---4)

**来源**: Netflix Tech Blog

**摘要**: <p>By <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/praneethy91/">Praneeth Yenugutala</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/victor-dusa/">Victor Dusa</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/anastasia-samoilova01/">Anastasia Samoilova</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/megan-ren-7b78a81a8/">Megan Ren</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/justinwei96/">Justin Wei</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/mayworm/">Marcelo Mayworm</a></p><p>At Netflix, data and machine learning practitioners run millions of workloads each day across orchestration systems and compute engines such as Titus, Spark, Trino, and Druid. On peak days, roughly 100,000 workloads fail. When workloads fail, the failure itself is only the beginning. Someone still needs to find the useful signal in several logs, decide whether the cause sits in their code or the platform, and determine whether to retry, reconfigure, or escalate. Even a small share of difficult failures can consume engineering time, waste compute, and interrupt critical data pipelines.</p><p>Our first answer to that problem was <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/auto-diagnosis-and-remediation-in-netflix-data-platform-5bcc52d853d1">Pensive</a>, an auto-diagnosis and remediation system in Netflix’s data platform. Pensive collected logs from failed workloads, used regular expressions and hand-authored Java rules to extract and classify errors, then passed retry or remediation guidance to products such as <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/maestro-netflixs-workflow-orchestrator-ee13a06f9c78">Maestro</a>. It made ~ 50% of failures self-service and, in some cases, automatic.</p><p>Rules work well for known patterns, but their coverage grows only when someone recognizes a new failure mode, understands the logs, writes a rule, and keeps it current as engines and log formats change. Over one three-month period, Pensive processed roughly seven million job-failure classifications, yet about half fell into the unknown bucket. We needed a way to expand coverage without giving up the deterministic parts of Pensive that already worked.</p><p>To solve this ambiguous and complex problem at Netflix scale, we invested in Pensive AI to add AI-assisted error extractions and focused diagnosis and remediation guidance to our data and ML practitioners. The goal was not simply to place a model in front of a large collection of logs. We needed bounded extraction, predictable evaluation inputs, responsive product behavior, and quality checks that could follow the system from local development through production. Those requirements shaped every layer of the design, which we will discuss in this blog. Pensive AI wraps AI around deterministic processes, which gives broader coverage, low latency, controlled AI costs, and easier maintenance across the software development lifecycle. Building on this foundation, we plan to explore more agentic debugging experiences, as Part 6 describes.</p><p>Early results are encouraging. AI error extraction now covers 100% of failures, including the failures that legacy Pensive classified as unknown. Failed step attempts where no one used Pensive AI averaged 321% more manual debug-log exploration actions than attempts where someone did (an observational comparison, not a causal claim).</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*2TSVpCdPYj4fMMlFPuheCg.png" /><figcaption><em>Figure 1. Pensive AI architecture. Always-on error extraction and on-demand debugging complement each other and share log sources, the LLM gateway, tracing, and the cache. Archived logs feed offline evals and release gates.</em></figcaption></figure><p>The two paths complement each other. Error extraction improves an existing capability and is always on. It lets the user of a failed Maestro workload quickly check the parts of the logs that may point to an immediate root cause. It has low latency and low cost, and it can help in a large share of cases. On-demand debugging enables a new capability. It looks at symptoms across the logs and adds the LLM’s own knowledge and reasoning for a deeper analysis, at slightly higher latency and cost. Extraction gives a fast first look at every failure, and on-demand debugging triggered by the user goes deeper when that first look is not enough.</p><h3>Part 1: Finding the useful errors</h3><p>Legacy Pensive rules are precise, but each one captures a particular log shape. A simplified rule looks like this:</p><pre>- name: SparkContainerOOM<br />logSource: GENIE_STDERR<br />restartable: false<br />regex: &quot;Container.*is running beyond physical memory limits.*Killing container&quot;<br />summary: Spark AM container runs out of memory</pre><p>With Pensive AI, we wanted to find useful errors that had no corresponding rule and adapt more easily when familiar errors changed shape after a platform upgrade. Data Portal (our UI for data pipelines) surfaces these extracted errors when available and links back to the source logs, giving practitioners a focused starting point for triage. That raises a question: how can we ask a model to identify an error without asking it to repeat a long stack trace token by token?</p><p>Producing output tokens is much slower than reading input tokens. A direct transcription approach would make long errors expensive and slow. Instead, Pensive numbers the lines of each log and gives the model a sequence of {lineNumber, text} records. The model identifies the first and last lines of the error span, and Pensive reconstructs the selected text from its own log copy, leading to lower cost and better latency. This way, the model always returns two integers (start and end line number), whether the selected logs span a few lines or hundreds of lines. The displayed content comes directly from the original log, reducing hallucinations. The model can still choose the wrong range, which is why extraction quality remains an integral measurement for our AI evals, as we will discuss later.</p><p>For orchestrated workflows, “Pensive AI Error Extraction” runs automatically whenever a step fails. A step attempt is one execution of a step within a Maestro workflow. Maestro already calls Pensive for a regex-derived error and restartability classification, so AI extraction runs in the same request and returns its results alongside the established fields. The overall Pensive response must return within Maestro’s hard 90-second post-processing timeout for a failed step attempt.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*LwR_-PjFMZiZzmw8QrkyzQ.png" /><figcaption><em>Figure 2. Error extraction. Legacy rules always run. The AI call is bound by a shared deadline, and late calls are excluded. Pensive merges both results into one response and caches it.</em></figcaption></figure><p>That deadline shaped the extraction path. Pensive extracts errors from the logs in parallel to reduce latency, and the per-log calls share one deadline set well inside Maestro’s timeout limit. A per-log extraction call that misses the deadline is excluded from the result, allowing the overall request to return on time.</p><p>The data contract between Pensive AI and the various consumers needed the same care. The legacy data model contained one flattened error string; AI extraction instead returns a list of errors, one for each log source, with deep links back to the originating logs. Changing the old field in place would have broken existing clients. We introduced the new data model beside the legacy non-AI error extraction data model and continued producing both. Data Portal, our user interface for monitoring and observing data pipelines, preferred AI errors when available and fell back to the legacy value when they were not during the rollout. That allowed a gradual rollout and ensured that historical failures still display an error, since we chose not to backfill the old data due to log retention issues.</p><p>The migration is still in progress today. The Data Portal UI surface used for failed Maestro steps reads the new AI extracted error field today, while the query editor (for editing and running SQL), Workbench notebooks (managed compute platform and UI to edit and run jupyter based notebooks) and some slack clients calling Pensive still use the legacy error extraction path. Each client needs a compatible schema change before it can consume the new data. Keeping both fields populated lets teams adopt the new behavior at their own pace without forcing a coordinated cutover as we continue evolving Pensive AI.</p><h3>Part 2: Full failure diagnosis and the product experience</h3><p>Beyond error extraction, we built Pensive AI Debugging, a tool that analyzes failed step attempts and provides root-cause analysis and suggested remediations. It is available through programmatic APIs, MCP, and the product UI (Data Portal), all backed by a common entry point across the Netflix Data Platform. This shared model supports consistent behavior and custom product experiences.</p><p>This diagnosis still has to arrive quickly and remain consistent across investigations. A debugging response can take tens of seconds or a minute to generate, compared with a few seconds for error extraction. We run full AI-based debugging on demand instead of automatically, which keeps costs under control because most failed steps are never opened. We treat the failed step attempt, not the person who triggered it, as the unit of work, so everyone investigating the same failed attempt sees the same diagnosis.</p><p>That choice gives us a stable unit of work for caching. We cache each debugging response by step attempt. A second request becomes a key-value read instead of another AI inference call. The cache also prevents duplicate work, reducing latency and lowering costs further. This also means that a debugging generated in the Data Portal is cached and immediately retrieved for the MCP path instead of incurring another AI inference call. A conditional write creates an in-progress record, one caller starts generation, and other callers wait for the shared result to avoid conflicts when concurrent requests come in for the same step attempt failure. If generation remains in progress past an abandonment timeout, Pensive treats the record as abandoned and reclaims it. Completed responses expire after 90 days to help control costs and optimize storage because debugging requests for much older workloads are uncommon.</p><p>Pensive AI supports multiple ways to initiate debugging responses. A caller can (1) read a cached response, (2) generate one if no cache entry exists, or (3) regenerate and overwrite an existing response. Those semantics support the product UI, programmatic access, and the evaluation harness while keeping the response associated with a single immutable workload failure.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*2TdUH-b7FY1G2AvfHVu4QA.png" /><figcaption><em>Figure 3. On-demand debugging. Three client actions share one entry point. A conditional write claims the step attempt. A cache hit returns the cached result. A won claim generates and streams a response, and the result persists even if the client disconnects.</em></figcaption></figure><p>For users, the most important behavior is that a long-running answer feels alive. Pensive streams the response as it is produced. The service emits server-sent events, and the GraphQL layer republishes them as a WebSocket subscription for the Data Portal client.</p><p>Each event carries the complete response generated so far. That duplicates a small amount of text, but it keeps the client simple: it does not need a replay buffer or token reassembly logic, and the next event corrects the display after a dropped or late event. Generation is independent of the connection that requested it. A disconnect does not cancel the work, and the final answer still reaches the cache. A hard reload can show a loading state and poll for the completed result. Within the single-page application backing the Data Portal UI, active streams are maintained independently of view and component lifecycle, so navigation does not interrupt a response in progress.</p><h3>Part 3: Measuring and improving the behavior we want</h3><p>LLM-assisted systems need more than a demo or a few working examples. Before shipping in production, we need confidence in both error extraction and debugging guidance, and we need to know that we have not regressed on failures that legacy Pensive already handled. We built separate evaluation frameworks focused on the two distinct jobs.</p><p>Building labeled data is often the expensive part of evaluation. For error extraction, legacy Pensive’s hand-written rules provide reference labels for failures they recognize. They also serve as excellent ground truth in most cases. We run those rules over production logs and compare their extracted spans with Pensive AI’s output. This gives us a reliable regression set without relabeling every example by hand, though it is not an absolute ground truth (since our human-built rules could also have been stale). For now, we keep these rules frozen as a label source. This lets us continue to build comparison evals and run them on top of the existing system.</p><p>Stable inputs are essential for repeatable evaluation experiments where we A/B test new models/configurations or context injections to improve response quality. The logs Pensive AI uses can expire after as few as seven days or as many as 30 days. Production Pensive AI requests archive fetched logs to S3 object storage asynchronously during workload execution time so the upload is not on the user-facing critical path. Uploads do not overwrite an existing object, preserving the exact snapshot used to create an evaluation case. Evals use those archived logs, so score changes remain attributable to the system under test. The archive also prevents parallel experiments from overloading the source systems and lets us run them without affecting production. Cached S3 log inputs further reduce costs when running evaluations.</p><p>Concretely, we measure four complementary signals for error extraction:</p><ul><li><strong>Inclusion:</strong> An LLM judge compares the legacy Pensive error for a failure with the errors Pensive AI extracted and determines whether the known error is still represented, even when wording differs.</li><li><strong>Precision:</strong> A second judge sees only the extracted AI errors and evaluates whether each is meaningful and actionable on its own.</li><li><strong>Repeated noise:</strong> A script looks across the whole dataset for lines that recur too often. A generic status line may look harmless in one answer but become distracting if it appears in a large share of failures. Lines appearing in more than about 30% of workflows are candidates for explicit exclusion. In practice, the exclusion list has only three lines, each of which otherwise appeared in nearly every extracted error.</li><li><strong>Latency:</strong> We record the latency of every extraction call. Quality scores alone cannot reveal when an apparent improvement requires too much additional work, which often correlates with increased AI costs.</li></ul><p>This combination gives us an actionable feedback loop. Prompt changes can reduce general noise, while the dataset-level view exposes the stubborn boilerplate that prompt changes do not eliminate. Existing rules act as a regression floor, and archived logs give us a repeatable way to compare changes. At Pensive AI’s scale, model and system choices must satisfy quality, latency, maintainability and cost constraints together.</p><p>To seed the debugging ground-truth dataset for evaluation, we mine internal engineering-support Slack threads and use an LLM to identify threads that link to an orchestrated workflow, describe a resolved problem, and match the class of issue Pensive AI handles. A second model synthesizes a reference debugging response from the relevant discussion. Paired with logs from that workflow failure, it becomes a concrete evaluation row in our evals dataset. This approach works only retrospectively, because Pensive AI in production aims to provide debugging guidance before such a Slack discussion happens.</p><p>We also made the debugging generation process visible. Tracing follows the request through Pensive, with child spans for each log fetch and extraction tool call. Our tracing backend based on Braintrust brings the prompt, completion, tool calls, and latency into a single view. During early product development, that visibility helped us inspect low-performing debugging responses in context instead of guessing which input or operation caused them. Tracing helped us improve the responses and fill context gaps even before we had comprehensive evals. In production, the API and UI return the trace identifier with the response, so feedback can lead directly to the trace for in-depth analysis.</p><h3>Part 4: Keeping quality in the release path</h3><p>Evaluation is not only for experiments with LLM orchestration, models, prompts, or context changes. Changes to log selection, trimming logic, or a source system can change what the model sees and therefore change extraction quality. We use the same evaluation harness and evaluators throughout local development and Pensive AI deployment so those effects are visible before users encounter them.</p><p>For each pull request, the code branch is deployed to an isolated preview environment and a CI job runs an experiment with our evals against that isolated environment. Precision and inclusion thresholds gate the merge. The job also runs after a build is promoted, catching interactions that were not present when individual pull requests were evaluated in isolation. If the promoted build misses its thresholds, it alerts the on-call engineer to investigate or roll back.</p><p>The evaluator runs on a schedule against production to surface gradual drift or external changes. Developers can also trigger evaluation experiments remotely while iterating, using the same deployed-environment path as CI. Consistent infrastructure makes the results comparable across local iteration, preview builds, promoted builds, and production.</p><h3>Part 5: Looking for feedback signal from users</h3><p>The system is only valuable if it reduces the amount of manual debugging and log chasing done by our users. We wanted a measurement that was available from product telemetry and close to the behavior Pensive AI was designed to change. Manual debug-log exploration became one of the main signals. The Pensive interface already offers links to logs for a failed step attempt. To estimate whether the feature reduced raw-log hunting, we compared failed step attempts where someone interacted with Pensive AI with those where no one did. The second group averaged 321% more manual debug-log exploration actions. This observational comparison does not establish causality or a specific reduction in mean time to resolution, but it suggests that focused diagnosis often allows practitioners to inspect fewer raw logs. Programmatic MCP access also lets teams build operational flows with their terminals or custom agentic experiences around the same diagnosis service.</p><p>Another more explicit signal we collect is thumbs up and thumbs down on the debugging response. These feedback signals land directly alongside the internal traces so we can quickly associate feedback to the complete log of inputs/outputs and the path the AI took to arrive at the answer. More importantly, we created an internal business-hours alerting system where each thumbs down response sends us a Slack alert. This was valuable to resolve a lot of rough edges and context gaps initially in the product and is helping us improve the product continuously even today.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*b_G74zKbN6phiKnMAcDSzA.png" /><figcaption><em>Figure 4. Feedback loop. A thumbs up or down joins the exact LLM trace that produced the response, by the same logId stamped during generation. Traces and feedback feed ground truth and eval datasets, and each thumbs down sends a Slack alert during business hours.</em></figcaption></figure><h3>Part 6: Future Work building on these foundations</h3><p>Pensive AI is the foundation for more work that goes beyond diagnosis and recommended remediations. We are exploring “Pensive AI Deepthink,” an opt-in managed agentic experience with higher cost and latency. It builds on the precise, low-latency analysis produced by Pensive AI Debugging and gathers additional context from internal documentation, manuals, past Slack discussions, source code, read-only warehouse queries, and Spark execution details. A routing step directs the investigation to the appropriate skill, such as Spark failures, data-audit failures, or late workflows. This keeps the shared starting point stable and deterministic while allowing the investigation to become more specialized, further improving recall. We are also in talks with the Spark team to leverage Pensive AI responses for failure debugging analysis and A/B comparison for upcoming platform migrations.</p><p>We also plan to invest in “Pensive AI Error Classification”. Hard-to-maintain legacy rules also classify failures by restartability and by whether they are platform or user caused. Maestro acts on those labels and changes its retry behavior accordingly like failing fast or tuning the default number of retries (platform errors are tried more than user errors by default). Error classifications must meet a higher bar than error extractions and debugging alone. Moving the majority of the error classifications to AI would let us retire most of the remaining rule-maintenance burden and avoid retrying unknown failures that were unlikely to succeed. For restartability, the costliest error is classifying a recoverable failure as non-restartable, because Maestro will turn a step that could have been recovered into a hard failure, leading to end user alerts and manual remediations. The evaluation framework will need to be tuned around that outcome before AI-enabled classifications can take on those decisions. Equally important, we must avoid retrying non-transient failures that will not succeed, because retries waste compute and delay data pipelines.</p><p>Our work on Pensive AI in Data Platform addresses concrete user pain points in debugging, improves maintainability for platform owners, and improves precision and recall for error extraction. Pensive AI extends coverage to failures that legacy Pensive previously classified as unknown. Reliability comes from the system around the AI model calls: bounded error extractions, compatible client data contracts, responsive delivery, reproducible evaluations, caching and tracing, and release controls that keep quality and cost visible as the platform changes.</p><h3>Part 7: Acknowledgements</h3><p>This work would not have been possible without our stunning colleagues across Data Platform and our Data Engineering and Analytics partners who provided early feedback. While we cannot name everyone individually, we are deeply grateful for their contributions, which shaped both response quality and the product experience. Special thanks to <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/adith-swaminathan-98198a21/">Adith Swaminathan</a> for advising on evaluations and inference improvements and acting as a thought partner. Thank you to the Orchestration team members, especially <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/rdeepak2002/">Deepak Ramalingam</a> and <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/malcolm-ramirez/">Malcolm Ramirez</a> for their additional engineering contributions.</p><p>For dogfooding and integrating Pensive AI as part of user support, we also thank the engineering support organization (ESO Team). We also thank Abhishek Kapatkar (Data Developer Experiences Team) and Emmie Dong (Data Movement Team) for their feedback to improve the Pensive AI integrations with the rest of the ecosystem. Special thanks to the AI Platform team (Kyle Ahn, Rob Mann) and Braintrust Team for helping with distributed tracing, evals infrastructure and model gateway runtime support.</p><p>We also thank Kamran Masood, Ely Spears, Ronald Leung, Anjali Norwood, Prashanth Ramdas, Charles Smith, Harikrishna Menon and other leaders for their constructive feedback and support on the Pensive AI project.</p><img alt="" height="1" src="https://medium.com/_/stat?event=post.clientViewed&amp;referrerSource=full_rss&amp;postId=6b0811a1c015" width="1" /><hr /><p><a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/making-failure-diagnosis-reliable-with-pensive-ai-6b0811a1c015">Making Failure Diagnosis Reliable with Pensive AI</a> was originally published in <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com">Netflix TechBlog</a> on Medium, where people are continuing the conversation by highlighting and responding to this story.</p>

### [Multi‑Ratio Video Resizing for Mobile Feed and Marketing](https://netflixtechblog.com/multi-ratio-video-resizing-for-mobile-feed-and-marketing-2e6b42810f49?source=rss----2615bd06b42e---4)

**来源**: Netflix Tech Blog

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/serdarcellat/">Serdar Cellat</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/yuchen-xie-788a3818/">Yuchen Xie</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/bernadette-marcu-b2980041/">Bernadette Marcu</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/blaidburgess/">Blaid Burgess</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/codymjarrett/">Cody Jarrett</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/cafpj/">Carlos Pereira</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/qing-shi-50a5a349/">Qing Shi</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/samuel-carvajal/">Samuel Carvajal</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/manuel-pamplona-b038a59a/">Manuel Pamplona</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/dorothyjung/">Dorothy Jung</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/feikoritsema/">Feiko Ritsema</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/benjamin-klein-usa/">Benjamin Klein</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/ajwoo/">Alex Woo</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/eugenelok/">Eugene Lok</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/anna-pulido-61025063/">Anna Pulido</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/rebeckaswe/">Rebecka Swe</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/sanford-holsapple/">Sanford Holsapple</a>, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/ranjithkumarshetty/">Ranjith Kumar Shetty</a></p><h3>Introduction</h3><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*4BdXCRIW5WXopLnt2qOBmg.jpeg" /><figcaption>Clips in the Netflix mobile app.</figcaption></figure><p>At Netflix, a crucial part of our mission is ensuring our content looks stunning on any device. Vertical formats are increasingly important for Netflix, as we have 1.2B followers across our social media accounts, and <a href="https://about.netflix.com/en/news/introducing-exciting-new-ways-to-find-and-enjoy-your-next-favorite-on-mobile">have recently rolled out Clips</a>, our vertical video feed in the Netflix mobile app. Traditionally, creating vertical assets meant editors and producers manually reframed horizontal videos in editing tools, a process that doesn’t scale as our catalog and use cases grow. To support both our in‑app feed and external marketing campaigns at scale, we built an automated multi‑ratio video cropping system, tightly integrated into our internal production tools, that can generate high‑quality vertical and other aspect‑ratio variants with minimal manual effort.</p><h3>Our Video Resizing Approach</h3><p>Converting cinematic 16:9 footage into mobile‑friendly aspect ratios takes more than a simple center crop. Filmmaking often involves dynamic character blocking and complex camera movement. We need an intelligent “virtual camera” that can follow the key action, respond to how each scene is composed, and produce crops that feel as intentional as the original shot.</p><p>We address this challenge by framing video resizing as a tracking and temporal optimization problem. Our pipeline processes each asset in three main stages, designed to produce 9:16, 4:5, and 1:1 crops in a single pass, without incurring additional computational overhead.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*Oh_reVhDMhLVxmfyo-VZaA.png" /><figcaption>An overview of the three-stage process converting horizontal footage into mobile aspect ratios.</figcaption></figure><h4>Shot Detection and Splitting</h4><p>First, we segment the video into atomic shots using a neural‑network–based shot boundary detector. In many cases, shots contain multiple characters conversing on opposite sides of the frame. To better handle these scenarios, we use an Active Speaker Detection based approach to logically split a single continuous shot into multiple sub‑shots. This allows our virtual camera to cut and re-focus on different speakers as the conversation flows. Our Active Speaker Detection leverages an internal face detector and LipFlap to reliably identify active speakers in both live‑action and animated titles.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/640/1*HFPP2af9zN9i-nJmBp95Cg.gif" /><figcaption>Shot splitting via Active Speaker Detection. The magenta box represents the dynamic vertical crop.</figcaption></figure><h4>Focus Region Discovery</h4><p>Once shots and sub‑shots are defined, we identify the most important region in each frame. We combine face detections with visual saliency maps to filter and prioritize the faces that should be in focus. In scenes with multiple characters, we further rank faces using signals such as active speaker status and relative size. Our model aggregates these cues with shot‑level content understanding to establish a continuous “focus region” for the virtual camera.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*rU5iHQ7-E5N1pytKrBaSTw.png" /><figcaption>Blue box shows the focus region around the primary character for reframing.</figcaption></figure><h4>Temporal Reframing</h4><p>Once we’ve established the focus region, the next challenge is deciding how the crop should move over time. Tracking the focus region on a strict frame-by-frame basis would produce a shaky, distracting viewing experience, so we apply a set of heuristics that consider how the focus region moves over the course of a shot. When its displacement is small, we lock a static crop window that consistently covers the relevant area. When there is significant motion, we compute a dynamic tracking window and smooth its path so that any panning feels intentional and cinematic.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/640/1*yrhOkvafGT5nk0cyHRwaCw.gif" /><figcaption>The vertical crop (magenta box) follows the character’s movement with a smooth panning path.</figcaption></figure><h3>Tooling and Workflow Integration</h3><p>We integrated the resizing algorithm directly into our internal Clips tool, so editors and producers can access it as part of their existing workflow. The interface enables them to automatically generate multiple aspect‑ratio versions of a clip with smart reframing. A unified in‑app module provides intuitive shot‑reframing controls, allowing users to review the algorithm’s crops, and quickly make targeted adjustments where needed.</p><p>For more complex shots or custom campaigns, producers can export the resizing parameters as XML and load them directly into external timeline editors such as Adobe Premiere. This allows agencies and external partners to start from the algorithm’s framing decisions instead of recreating crops manually or relying on tools like Premiere’s Auto Reframe. In our internal test, our resizing algorithm outperforms Auto Reframe on a curated evaluation set, so the XML export effectively brings those higher‑quality crops into external workflows. By bringing automated resizing and manual refinement into a single place, this pipeline significantly reduces production time and manual effort while preserving creative control.</p><figure><img alt="" src="https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1024/1*areusRDQUODlqQAwuTVqTA.png" /><figcaption>Video resizing interface with reframing controls</figcaption></figure><h3>Conclusion</h3><p>Our work on multi-ratio video resizing is one step in a broader effort to make content creation more scalable, without compromising creative intent. By pairing algorithm-driven reframing with editor-friendly tools, we’ve reduced the time teams spend on repetitive resizing tasks and unlocked faster experimentation across mobile feeds and marketing campaigns. As we continue to broaden these capabilities to new formats and workflows, our goal remains the same: to give creatives more time to tell great stories, and to deliver a consistently high-quality viewing experience for our members and fans.</p><img alt="" height="1" src="https://medium.com/_/stat?event=post.clientViewed&amp;referrerSource=full_rss&amp;postId=2e6b42810f49" width="1" /><hr /><p><a href="https://netflixtechblog.com/multi-ratio-video-resizing-for-mobile-feed-and-marketing-2e6b42810f49">Multi‑Ratio Video Resizing for Mobile Feed and Marketing</a> was originally published in <a href="https://netflixtechblog.com">Netflix TechBlog</a> on Medium, where people are continuing the conversation by highlighting and responding to this story.</p>

## LLM Infrastructure

### [v0.32.1rc0](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/releases/tag/v0.32.1rc0)

**来源**: vLLM GitHub Releases

**摘要**: <p>[Doc][RL] Document the weight-transfer HTTP protocol for trainers wit…</p>

---

### [v0.32.0](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/releases/tag/v0.32.0)

**来源**: vLLM GitHub Releases

**摘要**: <h1>v0.32.0</h1>
<h2>Highlights</h2>
<p>This release features 814 commits from 363 contributors (121 new)!</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Structured decisions and generic parsers</strong>: the new <code>POST /v1/systemone</code> endpoint answers <code>choice</code>, <code>noul</code> and <code>score</code> questions over a structured state (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59299">#59299</a>), is served by default (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60677">#60677</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60696">#60696</a>), and also runs on <code>Cloudflare/clef-omni</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/61064">#61064</a>), <code>LiquidAI/d1-3B</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60676">#60676</a>) and DiffusionGemma (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60466">#60466</a>). The new <code>hf</code> tool and reasoning parser (<code>--tool-call-parser hf</code>, <code>--reasoning-parser hf</code>) reads the checkpoint's <code>response_template</code>, so models that ship it need no model-specific parser (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58604">#58604</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58602">#58602</a>), in both the Python and Rust frontends (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59005">#59005</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Low-precision KV cache everywhere</strong>: NVFP4 KV cache now runs on SM8x and SM12x (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/46963">#46963</a>), SM90 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60620">#60620</a>) and with XQA decode on SM12x (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60452">#60452</a>), and on MiniMax-M3 sparse attention on SM100 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59300">#59300</a>); GLM-5.x defaults to an FP8 KV cache on SM100, halving KV memory per rank with 2.3-5.5% more throughput at a 7-11% TTFT cost in the PR's benchmark (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60140">#60140</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60281">#60281</a>); <code>fp8_ds_mla</code> on SM90 gives GLM-5.3-Flash up to 31% more output throughput (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59246">#59246</a>); ROCm gains the 4-bit <code>ultraquant_4bit</code> KV cache with about 2x the density of FP8 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57057">#57057</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Model Runner V2</strong>: a GPU n-gram speculator with variable-length drafts (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40704">#40704</a>), Elastic EP (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53934">#53934</a>), pipeline parallelism with prefill context parallelism for MLA models (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59139">#59139</a>), a stock torch.compile mode (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59079">#59079</a>), and MRV2 is now the default on ROCm with the model-specific MRV1 exceptions removed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56360">#56360</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Faster startup and serving</strong>: <code>vllm download-kernels</code> installs FlashInfer's precompiled kernels (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58765">#58765</a>); <code>vllm preload --preload-autotune</code> keeps the FlashInfer autotune table warm across restarts (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60085">#60085</a>); a shared-memory tensor arena broadcasts large multimodal tensors to workers with one copy (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51207">#51207</a>); tokenizers and processors load from the local snapshot (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60825">#60825</a>); and with <code>--api-server-count</code> above 1 each connection goes to the least-loaded API worker, for 43-85% more req/s on short requests (Qwen3-0.6B, 32 input / 1 output tokens) in the PR's benchmark (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60723">#60723</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Model support</strong>: new EmbeddingGemma 2 omni-modal embeddings (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60254">#60254</a>), MiniCPM-V 4.7 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58674">#58674</a>) and Nemotron 3.5 ASR (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59827">#59827</a>); video input in the Transformers modeling backend (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57441">#57441</a>); GLM-5.3 sparse MLA index conversions reused across layers (3.5-3.9x on that kernel, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59464">#59464</a>) and fused multi-step draft decode (13.3% at concurrency 1, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57443">#57443</a>); HiSparse per-request residency caching for up to 58% more throughput on GLM-5.3-NVFP4 on B200 with long, fully GPU-resident contexts at high concurrency (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60083">#60083</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Security</strong>: fixes for <a href="https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-4hhp-h66f-j5j7" title="GHSA-4hhp-h66f-j5j7">GHSA-4hhp-h66f-j5j7</a> (chat template resource exhaustion, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/50300">#50300</a>), <a href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/security/advisories/GHSA-q97c-p368-75c4" title="GHSA-q97c-p368-75c4">GHSA-q97c-p368-75c4</a> (tokenizer <code>max_token_id</code>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59491">#59491</a>) and <a href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/security/advisories/GHSA-frvg-qw88-hww9" title="GHSA-frvg-qw88-hww9">GHSA-frvg-qw88-hww9</a> (P2P ZMQ socket exhaustion, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51504">#51504</a>); CORS no longer allows every origin by default (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/61009">#61009</a>); untrusted images are limited to raster formats (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60022">#60022</a>); shared-memory multimodal cache handles are signed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59357">#59357</a>); and JSON schemas nested deeper than 64 levels are rejected (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60036">#60036</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Breaking changes</strong>: Python 3.10 dropped (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60402">#60402</a>); default CORS origins restricted (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/61009">#61009</a>); <code>transformers&gt;=5.16.1,&lt;5.20.0</code> and <code>tokenizers&gt;=0.23.2</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57387">#57387</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59796">#59796</a>); torch.compile sequence parallelism and AsyncTP passes removed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/60517">#60517</a>); cascade attention removed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/61126">#61126</a>); <code>mamba_cache_mode="all"</code> removed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58997">#58997</a>); <code>max_tokens</code> no longer reserved from the context window (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57035">#57035</a>); Responses API reasoning stream events renamed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59652">#59652</a>).</li>
</ul>

<h3>Other Artifacts</h3>
<p>Pre-built release artifacts are available in the <strong>Assets</strong> section at the bottom of this page, including:</p>
<ul>
<li>Source distribution tarball</li>
<li>CUDA 12.9 Python wheels for x86_64 and arm64</li>
<li>CUDA 13.0 Python wheels for x86_64 and arm64</li>
